<!-- theorem-1: body_md -->
\label{thm:unified-sobolev-rates}
Let $m\ge1$, and let $A_0$ be one of the three model generators treated
below. Let $p\in\{2,3\}$ denote its principal differential order, and
let $\mathcal J$ denote the identity in the parabolic case and the
normalizing conjugation in the two nonsmoothing cases. Write
\[
A=\mathcal J A_0\mathcal J^{-1},
\]
and let $\mathcal M_{2m,n}(t)$ be the depth-$m$ Richardson multiproduct
associated with the centered-difference Chernoff family for $A$.

Under the coefficient and stability hypotheses of the corresponding
model, the following hold.

\emph{(a) Analytic-semigroup smoothing case.}
For
\[
A_0=aD^2+bD+c,
\qquad p=2,
\]
and every $\delta>0$, put
\[
\eta_\delta:=\min\{\delta/2,1\}.
\]
Then there exist constants $C_{m,\delta},\Omega_{m,\delta}\ge0$ such
that
\[
\|\mathcal M_{2m,n}(t)f-e^{tA_0}f\|_{L^2}
\le
C_{m,\delta}e^{\Omega_{m,\delta}t}
\frac{t^{2m+\eta_\delta}}{n^{2m}}
\|f\|_{H^{2m+\delta}},
\qquad
0<t\le h_0n.
\]
Thus every datum in $H^{pm+\delta}=H^{2m+\delta}$ is admissible at
depth $m$.

\emph{(b) Nonsmoothing group case.}
For the Schr\"odinger-type model
\[
A_0=iaD^2+bD+c,
\qquad p=2,
\]
and the third-order dispersive model
\[
A_0=aD^3+bD+c,
\qquad p=3,
\]
there exist constants $C_m,\Omega_m\ge0$ such that
\[
\left\|
\mathcal J^{-1}\mathcal M_{2m,n}(t)\mathcal J f
-
e^{tA_0}f
\right\|_{L^2}
\le
C_me^{\Omega_mt}
\frac{t^{2m+1}}{n^{2m}}
\|f\|_{H^{(p+2)m}},
\qquad
0\le t\le h_0n.
\]
Hence the safe complete-word datum scale is
\[
H^{(p+2)m},
\]
which gives respectively
\[
H^{4m}
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
H^{5m}.
\]

In all three cases the fixed-depth Richardson convergence rate is
therefore
\[
O(n^{-2m}).
\]
<!-- end theorem-1 -->
<!-- theorem-2: body_md -->
\label{thm:unified-analytic-rate}
Let $A_0$ be any of the three model generators above, let
$p\in\{2,3\}$ be its principal differential order, and assume the
analytic hypotheses of the corresponding model. Let
$0<\rho_*<\rho_0$ and $T>0$ be fixed.

Then there exist constants $C_T,K_T,\Gamma\ge0$ such that the
depth-$m$ product expansion satisfies
\[
C^{\rm an}_{m,T}
\le
C_TK_T^m\Gamma((p+1)m+1).
\]
After Richardson cancellation, there exist constants
$C_T,K_*>0$ such that
\[
\|\mathcal M_{2m,n}(t)f-e^{tA}f\|_{\rho_*}
\le
C_Te^{\Gamma t}t
\left(
\frac{K_*t^{2}m^{p-1}}{n^2}
\right)^m
\|f\|_{\rho_0},
\qquad
0\le t\le T.
\]
Consequently, choosing
\[
m(n)=\left\lfloor\eta n^{2/(p-1)}\right\rfloor
\]
with $\eta>0$ sufficiently small, there exist constants
$C_T,c_T>0$ such that, for all sufficiently large $n$,
\[
\|\mathcal M_{2m(n),n}(t)f-e^{tA}f\|_{\rho_*}
\le
C_Te^{\Gamma t}t\,
e^{-c_Tn^{2/(p-1)}}\|f\|_{\rho_0},
\qquad
0\le t\le T.
\]
After conjugating back when necessary, the same rate holds for the
original generator $A_0$. In particular,
\[
p=2
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
e^{-cn^2},
\]
for the parabolic and Schr\"odinger-type models, while
\[
p=3
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
e^{-cn},
\]
for the third-order dispersive model.
<!-- end theorem-2 -->
<!-- theorem-3: body_md -->
\label{thm:unified-sobolev-rates}
Let $A_0$ be one of the following three model
generators.

\smallskip

\noindent
\emph{(P) Parabolic second order:}
\[
A_0=aD^2+bD+c,
\]
with 
\[
a\in W^{2m+4,\infty}(\mathbb R),\qquad
b\in W^{2m+3,\infty}(\mathbb R),\qquad
c\in W^{2m+2,\infty}(\mathbb R).
\]

\smallskip

\noindent
\emph{(S) Schr\"odinger-type second order:}
\[
A_0=iaD^2+bD+c,
\]
with
\[
a\in W^{4m+2,\infty}(\mathbb R),\qquad
b\in W^{4m+1,\infty}(\mathbb R),\qquad
c\in W^{4m,\infty}(\mathbb R).
\]
                                                             
                                                              
                           
   
                          
       
                        
   

\smallskip

\noindent
\emph{(D) Third-order dispersive:}
\[
A_0=aD^3+bD+c,
\]
with 
\[
a\in W^{5m+3,\infty}(\mathbb R),\qquad
b\in W^{5m+1,\infty}(\mathbb R),\qquad
c\in W^{5m,\infty}(\mathbb R).
\]

Let $p\in\{2,3\}$ denote the differential order and let
$\mathcal M_{2m,n}(t)$ denote the depth-$m$ Richardson multiproduct
associated with the corresponding centered-difference Chernoff function.

Then the following hold.

\emph{(a). Parabolic model (P).}
For  every $\delta>0$, setting
\[
\eta_\delta:=\min\{\delta/2,1\},
\]
there exist constants $h_0, C_{m,\delta}, \Omega_{m,\delta}\ge0$ such that
\[
\|\mathcal M_{2m,n}(t)f-e^{tA_0}f\|_{L^2}
\le
C_{m,\delta}e^{\Omega_{m,\delta}t}
\frac{t^{2m+\eta_\delta}}{n^{2m}}
\|f\|_{H^{2m+\delta}},
\qquad
0<t\le h_0n.
\]

\emph{(b). Group cases (S) and (D).}
For the Schr\"odinger-type and third-order dispersive models, let
$\mathcal J$ denote the corresponding normalizing conjugation. Then
there exist constants $h_0, C_m,\Omega_m\ge0$ such that
\[
\left\|
\mathcal J^{-1}\mathcal M_{2m,n}(t)\mathcal J f
-
e^{tA_0}f
\right\|_{L^2}
\le
C_me^{\Omega_mt}
\frac{t^{2m+1}}{n^{2m}}
\|f\|_{H^{(p+2)m}},
\qquad
0\le t\le h_0n.
\]

Thus, at every fixed depth $m$, all three models converge at rate
\[
O(n^{-2m}),
\]
on the respective datum scales
\[
H^{2m+\delta},\qquad H^{4m},\qquad H^{5m}.
\]
<!-- end theorem-3 -->
<!-- theorem-4: body_md -->
\label{thm:unified-analytic-rate}

Let $A_0$ be one of
\[
aD^2+bD+c,
\qquad
iaD^2+bD+c,
\qquad
aD^3+bD+c,
\]
and set $p=2$ in the two second-order cases and $p=3$ in the
third-order case.

Assume that there exists $\rho>0$ such that
\[
a,b,c\in \mathcal{H}_{\rho},
\]
that $a$ and $b$ are real-valued on $\mathbb R$, and that
\[
a(x)\ge a_0>0,
\qquad x\in\mathbb R.
\]
{}

{}
For every $T>0$, there exists $C_T,c_T,K_*,\Gamma, N_T>0$ and $0<\rho_*<\rho$ such that for any natural $n>N_T$ and any $f\in \mathcal{J}^{-1}\mathcal{A}_{\rho_*}$:
$$
\left\|
\mathcal{J}^{-1}\mathcal M_{2m(n),n}(t)\mathcal{J} f-e^{tA_0} f
\right\|_{\rho_*}
\le
C_Te^{\Gamma t}t
e^{-c_Tn^{2/(p-1)}}
\|\mathcal{J} f\|_{\rho_*},
\qquad
0\le t\le T.
$$

Hence the two second-order models have rate
\[
e^{-c_T n^2},
\]
whereas the third-order model has rate
\[
e^{-c_T n}.
\]
<!-- end theorem-4 -->
<!-- theorem-5: body_md -->
\label{thm:unified-analytic-rate}
Let $A_0$ be one of the three model generators
\[
aD^2+bD+c,
\qquad
iaD^2+bD+c,
\qquad
aD^3+bD+c,
\]
and let
\[
p=
\begin{cases}
2,&\text{for the two second-order models},\\
3,&\text{for the third-order model}.
\end{cases}
\]
 
Assume that, for some $\varrho_0>0$,
\[
a,b,c\in\mathcal H_{\varrho_0},
\]
 $a$ and $b$ are real-valued on $\mathbb R$, and 
\[
a(x)\ge a_0>0,
\qquad x\in\mathbb R.
\]
Set
 
                                             
                                                
 
\begin{equation}
a_\infty:=\|a\|_{\mathcal H_{\varrho_0}},
\quad
r_a:=\frac{a_0\varrho_0}{4a_\infty},\quad 
   
 
                                       
   
M_p
=
\left(\frac2{a_0}\right)^{1/p},\quad 
   
   
m_p
=
a_\infty^{-1/p}
\cos\left(
\frac1p
\arccos\frac{a_0}{2a_\infty}
\right),
\label{eq:Mp-explicit} \ltx@label{eq:mp-explicit}
\end{equation}
and the Liouville distortion ratio
\begin{equation}
\gamma_p
:=
\frac{m_p}{M_p}
=
\left(
\frac{a_0}{2a_\infty}
\right)^{1/p}
\cos\left(
\frac1p
\arccos\frac{a_0}{2a_\infty}
\right)>0.
\label{eq:liouville-distortion} \ltx@label{eq:main-gamma-p}
\end{equation}
 
Fix $T>0$ and an initial analytic radius
\[
0<\rho_{\rm in}<r_a.
\]
In the parabolic case let $\kappa=0$ and $\gamma_p=1$, and  in the two nonsmoothing cases,
let $\kappa>0$ be a strip-loss constant.
                                              
for the normalized operator $\widetilde A_0$.

\noindent
Choose the final radius as follows:
   
                               
   
                                                                  
\begin{equation}
0<\rho_{\rm out}
<
\gamma_p e^{-\kappa T}\rho_{\rm in}.
\label{eq:original-compatible-radii}
\end{equation}
 
Then there exist constants
\[
C_T,c_T,\Gamma>0,
\qquad
N_T\in\mathbb N,
\]
and $\eta_T>0$ such that, with
\begin{equation}
m(n)
=
\left\lfloor
\eta_Tn^{2/(p-1)}
\right\rfloor,
\label{eq:main-growing-depth}
\end{equation}
one has
\begin{equation}
\left\|
\mathcal M_{2m(n),n}^{\rm orig}(t)f
-
e^{tA_0}f
\right\|_{\rho_{\rm out}}
\le
C_Te^{\Gamma t}\,
t\,
e^{-c_Tn^{2/(p-1)}}
\|f\|_{\rho_{\rm in}}\,,
\label{eq:main-original-analytic-rate}
\end{equation}
for every $n\ge N_T$, every $0\le t\le T$, and every
$f\in\mathcal A_{\rho_{\rm in}}$.

\noindent
Consequently, the parabolic and Schr\"odinger-type second-order
models converge with the rate
\[
O(e^{-c_Tn^2}),
\]
while the third-order dispersive model converges with the rate
\[
O(e^{-c_Tn}).
\]
<!-- end theorem-5 -->
<!-- remark-1: body_md -->
For $p\in\{2,3\}$, choose $r_p>0$ such that
\[
q_p:=a^{-1/p}\in H^\infty(S_{r_p}),
\qquad
m_p:=\inf_{S_{r_p}}\Re q_p>0,
\qquad
M_p:=\sup_{S_{r_p}}|q_p|<\infty.
\]
Then
\[
m_p|\Im z|
\le
|\Im\eta_p(z)|
\le
M_p|\Im z|,
\qquad z\in S_{r_p}.
\]
Consequently,
\[
\chi_p(S_{\rho_y})\subset S_{\rho_y/m_p},
\qquad
0<\rho_y<m_pr_p,
\]
and
\[
\eta_p(S_{\rho_x})\subset S_{M_p\rho_x},
\qquad
0<\rho_x<r_p.
\]

Hence, whenever
\[
0<\rho_y<m_p\rho_x,
\]
the normalizing map satisfies
\[
\mathcal J_p:\mathcal A_{\rho_x}\longrightarrow\mathcal A_{\rho_y},
\]
whereas, whenever
\[
0<M_p\rho_x<\rho_y,
\]
one has
\[
\mathcal J_p^{-1}:\mathcal A_{\rho_y}\longrightarrow\mathcal A_{\rho_x}.
\]
Both maps are bounded between the indicated analytic spaces.

Therefore, for every $T>0$ and every initial radius
$0<\rho_0^x<r_p$, any final radius satisfying
\[
0<\rho_*^x
<
\frac{m_p}{M_p}e^{-\kappa T}\rho_0^x
\]
is admissible.  More precisely, one may choose radii
\[
0<\rho_0^y<m_p\rho_0^x,
\qquad
0<\rho_1^y<\rho_0^y,
\]
and
\[
M_p\rho_*^x
<
\rho_*^y
<
\rho_1^ye^{-\kappa T}.
\]
<!-- end remark-1 -->
<!-- theorem-6: body_md -->
\label{thm:unified-analytic-rate}
Let $A_0$ be one of the three model generators above and let
$p\in\{2,3\}$ denote its principal differential order.

Assume that the coefficients are analytic in the following sense.

\emph{(P) Parabolic case.}
With
\[
\phi=b-a',
\qquad
p_0=c+\frac12(a''-b'),
\]
assume that $a$ is real,
\[
a(x)\ge a_0>0,
\qquad
a,\phi,p_0\in\mathcal W_R
\]
for some $R>0$. Choose $0<\rho_0<R$ sufficiently small that
\[
\rho_0 M_1(a;\rho_0)\le\frac{a_0}{2}.
\]

\emph{(S)--(D) Nonsmoothing cases.}
Assume that $a,b,c$ are such that the normalizing conjugation described
below transforms $A_0$ into
\[
A
=
c_pD^p+\frac12(vD+Dv)+w,
\qquad
c_2=i,\quad c_3=1,
\]
where $v$ is real and
\[
v,w\in\mathcal W_R
\]
for some $R>0$. Fix $0<\rho_0<R$.

Then, for every $T>0$ and every admissible smaller strip
$0<\rho_*<\rho_0$, the depth-$m$ product expansion satisfies
\[
C^{\rm an}_{m,T}
\le
C_TK_T^m\Gamma((p+1)m+1).
\]
Moreover, after Richardson cancellation,
\[
\|\mathcal M_{2m,n}(t)f-e^{tA}f\|_{\rho_*}
\le
C_Te^{\Gamma t}t
\left(
\frac{K_*t^2m^{p-1}}{n^2}
\right)^m
\|f\|_{\rho_0},
\qquad
0\le t\le T.
\]

Consequently, for $\eta>0$ sufficiently small and
\[
m(n)=\left\lfloor\eta n^{2/(p-1)}\right\rfloor,
\]
there exist constants $C_T,c_T>0$ such that, for all sufficiently large
$n$,
\[
\|\mathcal M_{2m(n),n}(t)f-e^{tA}f\|_{\rho_*}
\le
C_Te^{\Gamma t}t\,
e^{-c_Tn^{2/(p-1)}}\|f\|_{\rho_0},
\qquad
0\le t\le T.
\]
After conjugating back when necessary, the same rate holds for the
original generator $A_0$.

Hence
\[
p=2
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
e^{-cn^2},
\]
for the parabolic and Schr\"odinger-type models, whereas
\[
p=3
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
e^{-cn},
\]
for the third-order dispersive model.
<!-- end theorem-6 -->
<!-- definition-1: body_md -->
\label{def:c0-semigroup}
A family
$(T(t))_{t\ge0}\subset\mathcal L(X)$ is a semigroup if
\[
 T(0)=I,\qquad T(t+s)=T(t)T(s).
\]
 
The family is called strongly continuous if $\lim_{t\to0^+}T(t)f=f\quad(f\in X)$.\\
 
A strongly continuous semigroup is called \emph{$C_0$-semigroup} or \emph{evolution semigroup} or \emph{evolution operator} or \emph{dynamical system}.\\
 
Its generator is
\[
 Af=\lim_{t\to 0^+}\frac{T(t)f-f}{t},\quad \mbox{with domain}\quad
 D(A)=\left\{f\in X: \lim_{t\to 0^+}\frac{T(t)f-f}{t} \text{ exists}\right\}.
\]
<!-- end definition-1 -->
<!-- definition-2: body_md -->
\label{def:closed-operator}
                                    
 
   
                                 
   
 
                                                
The operator $(A,D(A))$ is said to be \emph{closed} if its graph

\[
\mathcal G(A)
:=
\{(x,Ax):x\in D(A)\}
\subset X\times X
\]

is a closed subspace of \(X\times X\).

Equivalently, \(A\) is closed if, whenever a sequence \((x_n)_{n\geq1}\subset D(A)\) satisfies

\[
x_n\longrightarrow x
\quad\text{in }X,
\qquad
Ax_n\longrightarrow y
\quad\text{in }X,
\]

for some \(x,y\in X\), then

\[
x\in D(A)
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
Ax=y.
\]

When \(A\) is closed, its domain \(D(A)\), equipped with the graph norm

\[
\|x\|_{D(A)}
:=
\|x\|_X+\|Ax\|_X,
\]

is itself a Banach space.
<!-- end definition-2 -->
<!-- remark-2: body_md -->
\label{rem:model-closures}
For each model we initially regard the differential expression on
\(C_c^\infty(\mathbb R)\subset L^2(\mathbb R)\). Under the coefficient assumptions
imposed below, these minimal operators are closable and their closures
are the natural Sobolev realizations
\[
\begin{aligned}
A\coloneqq aD^2+bD+c, \qquad D(\overline{A})&=H^2(\mathbb R),\\
A\coloneqq iaD^2+bD+c, \qquad D(\overline{A})&=H^2(\mathbb R),\\
A\coloneqq aD^3+bD+c, \qquad D(\overline{A})&=H^3(\mathbb R).
\end{aligned}
\]
                                             
We shall abuse notation and denote the closure of the operator $A$ again by $A$.
<!-- end remark-2 -->
<!-- theorem-7: body_md -->
\label{thm:acp-semigroup}
Let $(A,D(A))$ be a closed, densely defined linear operator on a Banach
space $X$, and assume that its resolvent set is nonempty: 
$
\rho(A)\neq\varnothing.
$
Then the following assertions are equivalent:
                  
      
                                                                   
                 
              
                                         
          
            
          
               
                                             
   
                        
       
                                         
   
 
      
                                                               
                         
                
\begin{enumerate}
\item
For every initial datum $f\in D(A)$, the abstract Cauchy problem \textup{(CP)}
                 
              
                                         
          
            
          
               
admits a unique classical solution, that is,
\[
u\in C^1([0,\infty),X),
\qquad
u(t)\in D(A)\quad\text{for every }t\ge0.
\]

\item
$(A,D(A))$ is the generator of a strongly continuous semigroup
$(T(t))_{t\ge0}$ on $X$.
\end{enumerate}
 
Whenever these equivalent conditions hold, the unique classical solution of the evolution associated with
\textup{(CP)} is represented by the semigroup orbit
\[
u(t)=T(t)f=e^{tA}f.
\]
<!-- end theorem-7 -->
<!-- definition-3: body_md -->
\label{def:chernoff-tangency}
A strongly continuous family
$S:[0,\infty)\to\mathcal L(X)$ with $S(0)=I$ is
\emph{Chernoff tangent} to $A$ on a core $\mathcal D\subset D(A)$ if
\[
 \lim_{h\to 0^+}\frac{S(h)f-f}{h}=Af,\qquad f\in\mathcal D.
\]
<!-- end definition-3 -->
<!-- theorem-8: body_md -->
\label{thm:cher-thm}
Let $S:[0,\infty)\to\mathcal L(X)$ be a family of bounded operator \emph{Chernoff tangent} to 
a closed linear (bounded or unbounded) operator $(A,D(A))$ which generate a $C_0$-semigroup $e^{t A}$, and 
\[
 \|S(h)^k\|_{\mathcal L(X)}\le Me^{\omega kh},\qquad
 h\ge0,\quad k\in\mathbb N,
\]
for some $M\ge 0$ and $\omega\in \mathbb R$. Then
\[
 e^{t A}f=\lim_{n\to \infty}S(t/n)^nf
\]
for every $f\in X$, uniformly for $t$ in compact subsets of
$[0,\infty)$.

Such a function $S$ is then called a Chernoff function for the operator $A$, and $S$ is called Chernoff-equivalent to $C_0$-semigroup $e^{t A}$.
<!-- end theorem-8 -->
<!-- theorem-9: body_md -->
\label{thm:general-richardson}
Assume that $\mathcal S$ satisfies the Chernoff conditions above and
that its products admit the depth-$m$ expansion \eqref{eq:product-expansion} on the
power scale $\boldsymbol\nu$.  Let $c_\ell$ satisfy \eqref{eq:general-richardson-system} and
define
\[
 \mathcal M_{\boldsymbol\nu,n}(t)
 :=
 \sum_{\ell=1}^m c_\ell
 \mathcal S\!\left(\frac{t}{j_\ell n}\right)^{j_\ell n}.
\]
Let $j_*:=\min_{1\le\ell\le m}j_\ell$. Then, for every
$f\in\mathcal D_m$ and every $0\le t\le h_0j_*n$,
\[
 \|\mathcal M_{\boldsymbol\nu,n}(t)f-T(t)f\|_X
 \le
 C'_m e^{\Omega_m t}
 \frac{t^{\nu_m+1}}{n^{\nu_m}}
 \|f\|_{\mathcal D_m},
\]
where $C'_m=C_m\sum_{\ell=1}^m|c_\ell|j_\ell^{-\nu_m}$.
<!-- end theorem-9 -->
<!-- proof-1: body_md -->
Apply \eqref{eq:product-expansion} with $N=j_\ell n$, multiply by $c_\ell$, and sum over
$\ell$.  The semigroup terms combine to $T(t)f$ because
$\sum_\ell c_\ell=1$, and the coefficient of
$n^{-\nu_r}E_{\nu_r}(t)f$ is
$\sum_\ell c_\ell j_\ell^{-\nu_r}=0$.  Hence
\[
 \mathcal M_{\boldsymbol\nu,n}(t)f-T(t)f
 =
 \sum_{\ell=1}^m c_\ell R^{\mathrm{prod}}_{m,j_\ell n}(t)f,
\]
and the pointwise bound in \eqref{eq:product-expansion} gives the asserted estimate.
<!-- end proof-1 -->
<!-- theorem-10: body_md -->
\label{thm:abstract}
Assume that $\mathcal S_2$ satisfies the Chernoff conditions above and
that its products admit the even expansion \eqref{eq:product-expansion} through depth
$m$, with $\nu_r=2r$.  For $j=1,\dots,m$, set
\begin{equation}
c_j^{(m)} = (-1)^{m-j}\,\frac{2j^{2m}}{(m-j)!\,(m+j)!},
\label{eq:even-richardson-weights}
\end{equation}
and define the multiproduct
\begin{equation}
\mathcal M_{2m,n}(t) := \sum_{j=1}^mc_j^{(m)}\,\mathcal S_2\!\left(\frac t{jn}\right)^{jn}.
\label{eq:even-richardson-multiproduct}
\end{equation}
Then for every $f\in\mathcal D_m$ and every $0\le t\le h_0n$,
\begin{equation}
\big\|\mathcal M_{2m,n}(t)f-e^{tA}f\big\|_X
\le C'_m e^{\Omega_m t}
\frac{t^{2m+1}}{n^{2m}}
\|f\|_{\mathcal D_m}.
\label{eq:even-richardson-rate}
\end{equation}
<!-- end theorem-10 -->
<!-- proof-2: body_md -->
Write $x_j=1/j^2$. By Lagrange interpolation formula, the solution of the system                                                                      
\begin{equation}
\sum_{j=1}^mc_j^{(m)}=1,\qquad
\sum_{j=1}^m\frac{c_j^{(m)}}{j^{2r}}=0,
\quad r=1,\dots,m-1.
\label{eq:even-richardson-moment-system}
\end{equation}
is given by the product formula
$c_j^{(m)}=\prod_{\ell\ne j}j^2/(j^2-\ell^2)$ which simplifies to \eqref{eq:even-richardson-weights}.


Applying \eqref{eq:product-expansion} with $N=jn$ and multiplying by $c_j^{(m)}$,
\[
c_j^{(m)}\mathcal S_2(t/(jn))^{jn}f = c_j^{(m)}e^{tA}f + \sum_{r=1}^{m-1}\frac{c_j^{(m)}E_{2r}(t)f}{j^{2r}n^{2r}} + c_j^{(m)}R^{\mathrm{prod}}_{m,jn}(t)f.
\]
Summing over $j=1,\dots,m$ and using \eqref{eq:even-richardson-moment-system}, the $e^{tA}f$ terms combine
to exactly $e^{tA}f$, and each of the $m-1$ intermediate sums
$\sum_jc_j^{(m)}j^{-2r}E_{2r}(t)f$ vanishes identically yielding:
\[
\mathcal M_{2m,n}(t)f - e^{tA}f = \sum_{j=1}^mc_j^{(m)}R^{\mathrm{prod}}_{m,jn}(t)f.
\]
Applying \eqref{eq:product-expansion}  at each $N=jn$ and $0\le t\le h_0n$,
\[
\|R^{\mathrm{prod}}_{m,jn}(t)f\|_X
\le C_m e^{\Omega_m t}
\frac{t^{2m+1}}{j^{2m}n^{2m}}
\|f\|_{\mathcal D_m}.
\]
Therefore
\[
\big\|\mathcal M_{2m,n}(t)f-e^{tA}f\big\|_X
\le C_m e^{\Omega_m t}
\frac{t^{2m+1}}{n^{2m}}
\Big(\sum_{j=1}^m\frac{|c_j^{(m)}|}{j^{2m}}\Big)
\|f\|_{\mathcal D_m},
\]
which is \eqref{eq:even-richardson-rate} with $C'_m=C_m\sum_j|c_j^{(m)}|j^{-2m}$.
<!-- end proof-2 -->
<!-- remark-3: body_md -->
\label{rem:recursive-richardson}
For an integer refinement factor $\rho\ge2$, the same cancellations may
be generated by
\[
 \mathscr R_{2k+2}^{(n)}(t)
 =\frac{\rho^{2k}\mathscr R_{2k}^{(\rho n)}(t)
        -\mathscr R_{2k}^{(n)}(t)}
 {\rho^{2k}-1},\qquad \mathscr R_2^{(n)}(t)=\mathcal S_2(t/n)^n.
\]
Then \eqref{eq:product-expansion} yields, pointwise in $t$,
\[
 \|\mathscr R_{2m}^{(n)}(t)f-e^{tA}f\|_X
 \le C_k e^{\Omega_m t}
 \frac{t^{2m+1}}{n^{2m}}\|f\|_{\mathcal D_m}.
\]
<!-- end remark-3 -->
<!-- proposition-1: body_md -->
\label{prop:resolvent-rate}
Assume the hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{thm:abstract},                                                                  
and let
$\widehat\omega_m:=\max\{\omega,\Omega_m\}$. Then, for every
$f\in\mathcal D_m$ and every $\lambda$ with
$\operatorname{Re}\lambda>\widehat\omega_m$,
\begin{equation}
\left\|\int_0^\infty e^{-\lambda t}\mathcal M_{2m,n}(t)f\,dt
-(\lambda I-A)^{-1}f\right\|_X
\le \frac{C_{\lambda,m}}{n^{2m}}\|f\|_{\mathcal D_m}.
\label{eq:richardson-resolvent-rate}
\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-1 -->
<!-- proof-3: body_md -->
Split the error integral at $h_0n$. On $[0,h_0n]$, \eqref{eq:even-richardson-rate} gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
&\int_0^{h_0n}e^{-\operatorname{Re}\lambda t}
 \|\mathcal M_{2m,n}(t)f-e^{tA}f\|_X\,dt\\
&\qquad\le
\frac{C'_m}{n^{2m}}
\int_0^\infty e^{-(\operatorname{Re}\lambda-\Omega_m)t}
 t^{2m+1}\,dt\,\|f\|_{\mathcal D_m},
\end{aligned}
\]
and the integral is finite because
$\operatorname{Re}\lambda>\Omega_m$.
On $[h_0n,\infty)$, the global stability bound \eqref{eq:multiproduct-global-error} gives
\[
 \int_{h_0n}^\infty e^{-\operatorname{Re}\lambda t}
 \|\mathcal M_{2m,n}(t)f-e^{tA}f\|_X\,dt
 \le C_{m,\lambda}e^{-(\operatorname{Re}\lambda-\omega)h_0n}\|f\|_X,
\]
                                                                               
together with the previous proves \eqref{eq:richardson-resolvent-rate}.
<!-- end proof-3 -->
<!-- proposition-2: body_md -->
\label{prop:UniStabCentDiff}
For every $h\ne0$ and every $f\in L^2(\mathbb R)$,
\begin{equation}
\operatorname{Re}\langle A_hf,f\rangle \le \omega\|f\|^2,
\label{eq:centered-generator-stability}
\end{equation}
 and consequently $\|e^{tA_h}\|\le e^{\omega t}$ for
all $t\ge0$.
<!-- end proposition-2 -->
<!-- proof-4: body_md -->
Since $\delta_h^*=-\delta_h$, $(\delta_h^{q})^*=(-1)^q\delta_h^{q}$ for
every integer $q$. Hence
\[
\begin{aligned}
\langle(-1)^{q-1}\delta_h^{q}p_{q}\delta_h^{q}f,f\rangle
&=(-1)^{q-1}(-1)^q
 \langle p_q\delta_h^qf,\delta_h^qf\rangle\\
&=-\|\sqrt{p_q}\,\delta_h^qf\|^2\le0 .
\end{aligned}
\]
                                                            
                                                                        
                                                                       
                                              
Every term of
$K(\delta_h)$ has the form
$M=\varphi\delta_h^j+\delta_h^j\varphi$ with $j$ odd and is
then skew-adjoint. Thus
\[
 \overline{\langle Mf,f\rangle}
 =\langle M^*f,f\rangle=-\langle Mf,f\rangle,
\]
so $\langle Mf,f\rangle$ is purely imaginary. Moreover, $\langle p_0f,f\rangle\le\omega\|f\|^2$
by hypothesis. Summing the three contributions gives \eqref{eq:centered-generator-stability}. Therefore, 
$\frac{d}{dt}\|e^{tA_h}f\|^2=2\operatorname{Re}\langle
A_he^{tA_h}f,e^{tA_h}f\rangle\le2\omega\|e^{tA_h}f\|^2$, and 
Gr\"onwall's inequality yields $\|e^{tA_h}f\|^2\le e^{2\omega t}\|f\|^2$.
<!-- end proof-4 -->
<!-- proposition-3: body_md -->
\label{prop:chernoff-tangency-even-structure}
Set
\[
\mathcal S_2(h):=e^{hA_h},\qquad h>0,
\qquad
\mathcal S_2(0):=I.
\]
Assume that there exists a common smooth core
                                      
                             
  
    
  
                            
  
                               
\[
\mathcal C\subset D(A^2),
\]
such that $
A\mathcal C\subset\mathcal C
$
and
$
\lim_{h\to 0}\|A_hg- Ag\|=0
$
for every \(g\in\mathcal C\). 
 
Then:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*)]
\item for every \(f\in\mathcal C\),
\[
\lim_{h\to 0}
\frac{\mathcal S_2(h)f-f}{h}
=
Af;
\]

\item for every integer $N\ge1$ and every $t\ge0$,
\[
\mathcal S_2(t/N)^N=e^{tA_{t/N}};                              
\]

\item for every finite \(M\),
\[
A_h
=
A+\sum_{r=1}^{M-1}h^{2r}R_r+h^{2M}Q_M(h)
\]
on a sufficiently regular Sobolev space, with a remainder uniformly
bounded between the corresponding scale spaces. In particular,
$
A_{-h}=A_h
$;

\item \(\mathcal S_2\) satisfies the Chernoff power bound
\eqref{eq:chernoff-power-bound}: for every \(h\ge 0\) and
\(k\in\mathbb N\),
\[
\|\mathcal S_2(h)^k\|
\le
e^{\omega kh},
\]
with the same \(\omega\) as in Proposition~\ref{prop:UniStabCentDiff}.
\end{enumerate}
<!-- end proposition-3 -->
<!-- proof-5: body_md -->
\emph{(i)} 
Fix \(f\in\mathcal C\).  Then, since \(A_h\) is bounded for each fixed
\(h\ne0\), the fundamental theorem of calculus gives\footnote{Observe that we can not use a norm Taylor expansion of
\(e^{hA_h}\), since  $\lim_{h\to 0}\|A_h\|=\infty$.}
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\frac{e^{hA_h}f-f}{h}-Af
&=
\int_0^1
e^{\theta hA_h}(A_hf-Af)\,d\theta+
\int_0^1
\bigl(e^{\theta hA_h}Af-Af\bigr)\,d\theta.
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:tangency-fundamental-identity}
\end{equation}

Letting $\omega_+:=\max\{\omega,0\}$, by Proposition~\ref{prop:UniStabCentDiff},
\[
\|e^{\theta hA_h}\|
\le
e^{\omega_+h},
\qquad 0\le\theta\le1,
\]
so that
\begin{equation}
\begin{aligned}
\lim_{h\to 0}\left\|
\int_0^1
e^{\theta hA_h}(A_hf-Af)\,d\theta
\right\|
&\le
\lim_{h\to 0} e^{\omega_+h}\|A_hf-Af\|=0.
\end{aligned}
\label{eq:tangency-first-term}
\end{equation}


We now prove the strong convergence
\[
e^{\theta hA_h}g\longrightarrow g
\]
uniformly for \(0\le\theta\le1\), for every \(g\in\mathcal C\).
Indeed,
\[
e^{\theta hA_h}g-g
=
\int_0^{\theta h}e^{sA_h}A_hg\,ds,
\]
and hence
\begin{equation}
\sup_{0\le\theta\le1}
\|e^{\theta hA_h}g-g\|
\le
h e^{\omega_+h}\|A_hg\|.
\label{eq:core-orbit-bound}
\end{equation}

Since $\lim_{h\to 0} \|A_hf-Af\|=0$, there exists \(h_0>0\) such that
\[
\|A_hg-Ag\|\le1,
\qquad 0<h\le h_0,
\]
so that
\begin{equation}
\sup_{0<h\le h_0}\|A_hg\|
\le
1+\|Ag\|<\infty.
\label{eq:core-generator-bound}
\end{equation}
Combining \eqref{eq:core-orbit-bound} and \eqref{eq:core-generator-bound},
\begin{equation}
\lim_{h\to 0}\sup_{0\le\theta\le1}
\|e^{\theta hA_h}g-g\|
\le \lim_{h\to 0}
h e^{\omega_+h}(1+\|Ag\|)=0.
\label{eq:core-strong-convergence}
\end{equation}

Now since \(f\in\mathcal C\) and \(A\mathcal C\subset\mathcal C\), then 
$
Af\in\mathcal C. 
$
Therefore applying \eqref{eq:core-strong-convergence} with \(g=Af\), yields
\[
\lim_{h\to 0}\sup_{0\le\theta\le1}
\|e^{\theta hA_h}Af-Af\|=0,
\]
so that
\[
\lim_{h\to 0}\left\|
\int_0^1
\bigl(e^{\theta hA_h}Af-Af\bigr)\,d\theta
\right\|
=0.
\]
Together with \eqref{eq:tangency-first-term}, this proves
\[
\boxed{
\lim_{h\to 0}
\frac{\mathcal S_2(h)f-f}{h}
=
Af.
}
\]

\smallskip
\noindent
\emph{(ii)} 
For \(h=t/N\),
\[
\mathcal S_2(t/N)^N
=
\bigl(e^{(t/N)A_{t/N}}\bigr)^N
=
e^{tA_{t/N}}.
\]

\smallskip
\noindent
\emph{(iii)} 
By \eqref{eq:centered-difference-expansion}, on every sufficiently regular Sobolev space,
\[
\delta_h
=
D+\sum_{r=1}^{M-1}
\frac{h^{2r}}{(2r+1)!}D^{2r+1}
+h^{2M}\mathcal R_{M,h},
\]
where \(\mathcal R_{M,h}\) is uniformly bounded between the
corresponding Sobolev spaces.

Substituting this expansion into  \eqref{eq:centered-generator} yields
\[
A_h
=
A+\sum_{r=1}^{M-1}h^{2r}R_r
+h^{2M}Q_M(h).
\]
                                                                      
                                                                        
                                                                       
                                                                   
                      

Moreover,
$
\delta_{-h}=\delta_h
$
yields
$
A_{-h}=A_h.
$


\smallskip
\noindent
\emph{(iv)} 
For every \(h>0\) and \(k\in\mathbb N\),
\[
\mathcal S_2(h)^k
=
(e^{hA_h})^k
=
e^{khA_h}.
\]
Proposition~\ref{prop:UniStabCentDiff} therefore gives
\[
\|\mathcal S_2(h)^k\|
=
\|e^{khA_h}\|
\le
e^{\omega kh}.
\]
For \(h=0\), the conclusion follows from
\(\mathcal S_2(0)=I\).
<!-- end proof-5 -->
<!-- lemma-1: body_md -->
\label{lem:defect-order}
Put $z=h^2$ and
\[
 \Delta(z):=\delta_{\sqrt z},\qquad z\ge0.
\]
Let $W_h$ be a finite word formed from $k$ occurrences of $\delta_h$,
separated by multiplication by sufficiently regular coefficient
functions:
                                                             
                                      
\[
 W_h=g_0\delta_h g_1\delta_h\cdots\delta_h g_k,
\]
where each $g_j$ acts by multiplication.  Assume that multiplication by
the $g_j$ is bounded on all Sobolev spaces occurring below.  Then, for
every integer $r\ge0$,
\begin{equation}
 \partial_z^r W_{\sqrt z}:H^s\longrightarrow
 H^{s-(2r+k)}
 \label{eq:defect-word-derivative}
\end{equation}
uniformly for $0\le z\le h_0^2$ and every $s\ge 2r+k$.

Consequently, 
                                                          
                                                                    
          
\[
 A_h=A+\sum_{r=1}^{m-1}h^{2r}R_r+h^{2m}Q_m(h)
\]
with
\begin{equation}
 R_r:H^s\longrightarrow H^{s-(2r+p)}
 \quad (s\ge 2r+p),
 \qquad
 Q_m(h):H^s\longrightarrow H^{s-(2m+p)}
 \quad (s\ge 2m+p),
 \label{eq:defect-order}
\end{equation}
uniformly for $0<|h|\le h_0$.
<!-- end lemma-1 -->
<!-- proof-6: body_md -->
The Fourier multiplier of $\Delta(z)$ is
\[
 i\frac{\sin(\sqrt z\,\xi)}{\sqrt z}
 =i\xi\,\phi(z\xi^2),
 \qquad
 \phi(y):=\frac{\sin\sqrt y}{\sqrt y},\quad \phi(0):=1.
\]
Hence, by the chain rule,
\begin{equation}
 \partial_z^q\!\left(i\xi\,\phi(z\xi^2)\right)
 =i\xi^{2q+1}\phi^{(q)}(z\xi^2).
 \label{eq:centered-symbol-derivative}
\end{equation}
For every fixed $q$, the function $\phi^{(q)}$ is bounded on
$[0,\infty)$.  Analyticity of $\phi$ at the origin gives boundedness of
all derivatives on a neighborhood of $0$.  For $y\ge1$, set
$r=\sqrt y$; since $d/dy=(2r)^{-1}d/dr$, each $y$-derivative introduces
an additional factor $r^{-1}$ before differentiating in $r$.  Repeated
differentiation of $\sin r/r$ therefore expresses $\phi^{(q)}(y)$ as
a finite linear combination of terms $r^{-a}\sin r$ and
$r^{-a}\cos r$ with $a>0$.  Hence $\phi^{(q)}$ is bounded on
$[1,\infty)$.
Therefore
\[
 \|\partial_z^q\Delta(z)f\|_{H^{s-(2q+1)}}
 \le C_q\|f\|_{H^s},
 \qquad 0\le z\le h_0^2.
\]
Consequently, the $q$th $z$-derivative of one centered-difference factor
costs at most $2q+1$ derivatives.

                                                                   
By Leibniz'
rule, $d^r W_h/dh^r$ is a finite sum indexed by
$q_1+\cdots+q_k=r$, and a typical summand contains
\[
 \partial_z^{q_1}\Delta(z),\ldots,
 \partial_z^{q_k}\Delta(z)
\]
and such summand's total Sobolev loss is at most
\[
 \sum_{j=1}^k(2q_j+1)=2r+k,
\]
which proves \eqref{eq:defect-word-derivative}.

Now suppose every word in $A_h$ contains at most $p$ centered
differences.  Taylor's formula in the variable $z=h^2$ gives
\[
 A_{\sqrt z}
 =A+\sum_{r=1}^{m-1}\frac{z^r}{r!}
   \left.\partial_z^rA_{\sqrt z}\right|_{z=0}
 +z^m Q_m(\sqrt z),
\]
where
\[
 Q_m(\sqrt z)
 =\frac1{(m-1)!}\int_0^1(1-\theta)^{m-1}
 \partial_z^mA_{\sqrt{\theta z}}\,d\theta.
\]
Applying \eqref{eq:defect-word-derivative} to each word yields \eqref{eq:defect-order}.
<!-- end proof-6 -->
<!-- proposition-4: body_md -->
\label{prop:sobolev-group-bound}
Let $p\in\{2,3\}$ and set\footnote{So that $c_pD^p$ is skew-adjoint on $L^2(\mathbb R)$.}
\[
 c_p=\begin{cases} i,&p=2,\\ 1,&p=3.\end{cases}
\]
 Let $k\in\mathbb N_{ 0}$,
$v\in W^{k+1,\infty}(\mathbb R,\mathbb R)$ and 
$w\in W^{k,\infty}(\mathbb R,\mathbb C)$.  On $H^p(\mathbb R)$
set
\[
 A=c_pD^p+vD+\tfrac12v'+w.
\]
Then $A$ generates a $C_0$-group on $L^2(\mathbb R)$.  Moreover, for
every integer $0\le j\le k$ there exists $\Omega_j\ge0$, depending
only on $j$, $\|v\|_{W^{j+1,\infty}}$, and
$\|w\|_{W^{j,\infty}}$, such that
\begin{equation}
 \|e^{tA}\|_{H^j\to H^j}\le e^{\Omega_j|t|},
 \qquad t\in\mathbb R.
\label{eq:sobolev-group-bound}
\end{equation}
<!-- end proposition-4 -->
<!-- proof-7: body_md -->
Write $A=A_p+B+w$, where $A_p=c_pD^p$ and
$B=vD+\tfrac12v'$.  The operator $A_p$ is skew-adjoint.  On
$H^p$, $B$ is skew-symmetric and is $A_p$-bounded with relative bound
zero: for every $\varepsilon>0$,
\[
 \|Bf\|_2
 \le \|v\|_\infty\|Df\|_2+\tfrac12\|v'\|_\infty\|f\|_2
 \le \varepsilon\|A_pf\|_2+C_{\varepsilon,v}\|f\|_2.
\]
                                                                
                                                           
                                                                     
                                                                      
                                                                             
               
                                          
                                                                               
 
Equivalently, $iA_p$ is self-adjoint and $iB$ is symmetric with
relative bound zero. Kato--Rellich therefore implies that
\[
G:=A_p+B
\]
is skew-adjoint on $L^2(\mathbb R)$ with domain $H^p(\mathbb R)$.
Hence, by Stone's theorem, $G$ generates a unitary $C_0$-group.

Let $W=M_w$ denote multiplication by $w$. Since
$w\in L^\infty(\mathbb C)$, one has
\[
W\in\mathcal L(L^2(\mathbb C)).
\]
The bounded perturbation theorem for $C_0$-semigroup generators,
applied first to $(G,W)$ and then to $(-G,-W)$, shows that both
\[
G+W
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
-(G+W)
\]
generate $C_0$-semigroups. Consequently,
\[
A=G+W=A_p+B+w,
\qquad D(A)=H^p(\mathbb R),
\]
generates a $C_0$-group on $L^2(\mathbb R)$.

For the Sobolev estimate, first take a smooth solution
$u(t)=e^{tA}u_0$ and set
\[
 E_j(t):=\sum_{r=0}^j\|D^ru(t)\|_2^2.
\]
The principal part commutes with $D^r$ and contributes zero to the
real part. Since $B$ is formally skew-symmetric on smooth vectors,
$\langle Bg,g\rangle=-\overline{\langle Bg,g\rangle}$ is purely
imaginary, and therefore $\operatorname{Re}\langle Bg,g\rangle=0$.
Moreover,
\[
 D^r(Bu)=B(D^ru)+[D^r,B]u,
\]
and Leibniz' rule gives
\[
 [D^r,B]u
 =\sum_{\ell=1}^r\binom r\ell v^{(\ell)}D^{r-\ell+1}u
 +\frac12\sum_{\ell=1}^r\binom r\ell
 v^{(\ell+1)}D^{r-\ell}u.
\]
Similarly,
\[
 D^r(wu)=wD^ru+[D^r,w]u,
 \qquad
 [D^r,w]u=\sum_{\ell=1}^r\binom r\ell
 w^{(\ell)}D^{r-\ell}u.
\]
Consequently
\[
 \bigl|\operatorname{Re}\langle [D^r,B]u,D^ru\rangle\bigr|
 +\bigl|\operatorname{Re}\langle [D^r,w]u,D^ru\rangle\bigr|
 \le C_r\bigl(\|v\|_{W^{r+1,\infty}}+
 \|w\|_{W^{r,\infty}}\bigr)E_j(t),
\]
while
$\operatorname{Re}\langle wD^ru,D^ru\rangle
\le\|w\|_\infty E_j(t)$.  Summing over $0\le r\le j$ yields
\[
 E_j'(t)\le 2\Omega_j E_j(t).
\]
Gr\"onwall gives the desired estimate for $t\ge0$; applying the same
argument to $-A$ yields the estimate for $t\le0$.  A standard density argument
extends the estimate from smooth data to $H^j$.
<!-- end proof-7 -->
<!-- lemma-2: body_md -->
\label{lem:half-derivative-commutator}
Let $\beta\in W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb R)$ be real-valued. Then                                            
\begin{equation}
\bigl\|
[\,|D|^{1/2},\beta\,]u
\bigr\|_{L^2}
\le
C
\bigl(
\|\beta\|_\infty\|\beta'\|_\infty
\bigr)^{1/2}
\|u\|_{L^2}.
\label{eq:half-commutator}
\end{equation}
Consequently,
\begin{equation}
\||D|^{1/2}(\beta u)\|_{L^2}
\le
\|\beta\|_\infty\||D|^{1/2}u\|_{L^2}
+
C
\bigl(
\|\beta\|_\infty\|\beta'\|_\infty
\bigr)^{1/2}
\|u\|_{L^2}.
\label{eq:half-product}
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-2 -->
<!-- proof-8: body_md -->
Set
\[
\Delta:=-D^2\ge0.
\]
Since
\[
|D|^{1/2}=\Delta^{1/4},
\]
the Laplace-transform representation of fractional powers gives \cite[Theorem~1]{Kwasnicki}:
\[
\Delta^{1/4}
=
c_{1/4}
\int_0^\infty
t^{-5/4}
\bigl(I-e^{-t\Delta}\bigr)\,dt
=
c_{1/4}
\int_0^\infty
t^{-5/4}
\bigl(I-e^{tD^2}\bigr)\,dt ,
\]
where $c_{1/4}=-1/{\Gamma(-1/4)}$. 
Hence
\[
[\,|D|^{1/2},\beta\,]
=
-c_{1/4}
\int_0^\infty
t^{-5/4}
[\,e^{tD^2},\beta\,]\,dt .
\]

Let
\[
G_t(x)
=
\frac1{\sqrt{4\pi t}}
e^{-x^2/(4t)}
\]
be the heat kernel. Then
\[
[\,e^{tD^2},\beta\,]u(x)
=
\int_\mathbb R
G_t(x-y)
\bigl(\beta(y)-\beta(x)\bigr)
u(y)\,dy.
\]
Young's inequality gives the two bounds
\[
\|[\,e^{tD^2},\beta\,]\|_{{L^2}\to {L^2}}
\le
2\|\beta\|_\infty
\]
and
\[
\|[\,e^{tD^2},\beta\,]\|_{{L^2}\to {L^2}}
\le
\|\beta'\|_\infty
\int_\mathbb R |z|G_t(z)\,dz
\le
C\sqrt t\,\|\beta'\|_\infty .
\]
Therefore
\[
\|[\,e^{tD^2},\beta\,]\|_{{L^2}\to{L^2}}
\le
C\min
\left\{
\|\beta\|_\infty,
\sqrt t\,\|\beta'\|_\infty
\right\}.
\]

Put
\[
\nu_0:=\|\beta\|_\infty,
\qquad
\nu_1:=\|\beta'\|_\infty.
\]
When $\nu_0,\nu_1>0$, split the  integral at
\[
t_0=(\nu_0/\nu_1)^2.
\]
Then
\begin{align*}
\|[\,|D|^{1/2},\beta\,]\|_{{L^2}\to{L^2}}
&\le
C \nu_1\int_0^{t_0}t^{-3/4}\,dt
+
C \nu_0\int_{t_0}^\infty t^{-5/4}\,dt
\\
&\le
C(\nu_0\nu_1)^{1/2}.
\end{align*}
The cases $\nu_0=0$ or $\nu_1=0$ are immediate.  This proves
\eqref{eq:half-commutator}; \eqref{eq:half-product} follows by writing
\[
|D|^{1/2}(\beta u)
=
\beta|D|^{1/2}u
+
[\,|D|^{1/2},\beta\,]u .
\]
<!-- end proof-8 -->
<!-- lemma-3: body_md -->
\label{lem:centered-transport-form}
Let $Q_h$ be skew-adjoint with symbol $iq_h(\xi)$ satisfying
\[
|q_h(\xi)|\le|\xi|,
\]
and put
\[
B_h(\beta)
=
\frac12(\beta Q_h+Q_h\beta).
\]
For real $\beta\in W^{1,\infty}$,
\[
\left|
\Re\langle iB_h(\beta)u,u\rangle
\right|
\le
C\|\beta\|_\infty
\||D|^{1/2}u\|_{L^2}^2
+
C
\bigl(
\|\beta\|_\infty\|\beta'\|_\infty
\bigr)^{1/2}
\||D|^{1/2}u\|_{L^2}\|u\|_{L^2},
\]
with $C$ independent of $h$.
<!-- end lemma-3 -->
<!-- proof-9: body_md -->
Set
\[
R_h:=-iQ_h.
\]
Then $R_h$ is self-adjoint and
\[
|R_h|\le |D|
\]
as Fourier multipliers.  Hence
\[
\left|
\Re\langle iB_h(\beta)u,u\rangle
\right|
\le
\||R_h|^{1/2}u\|_{L^2}\,
\||R_h|^{1/2}(\beta u)\|_{L^2}
\]
and therefore
\[
\le
\||D|^{1/2}u\|_{L^2}\,
\||D|^{1/2}(\beta u)\|_{L^2},
\]
whence applying Lemma~\ref{lem:half-derivative-commutator} concludes the proof.
<!-- end proof-9 -->
<!-- lemma-4: body_md -->
\label{lem:group-fixed-depth}
Let $p\in\{2,3\}$                                                              
 and suppose that
\[
 A=c_pD^p+vD+\tfrac12v'+w
\]
generates a $C_0$-group satisfying
\[
 \|e^{tA}\|_{H^j\to H^j}\le M_j e^{\Omega_j|t|},
 \qquad 0\le j\le (p+2)m.
\]
Assume moreover that the centered-difference propagators are uniformly
stable on $L^2$,
\[
 \|e^{tA_h}\|_{L^2\to L^2}\le M_0e^{\omega_0t},
 \qquad t\ge0,\quad 0<h\le h_0,
\]
and that, as provided by Lemma~\ref{lem:defect-order}, for every
$1\le k\le m$ one has the finite expansion
\[
 A_h=A+\sum_{r=1}^{k-1}h^{2r}R_r+h^{2k}Q_k(h),
\]
with
\[
 R_r:H^s\to H^{s-(2r+p)}
 \quad (s\ge 2r+p),
\]
and, uniformly for $0<h\le h_0$,
\[
 Q_k(h):H^s\to H^{s-(2k+p)}
 \quad (s\ge 2k+p).
\]
Then
\[
 e^{tA_h}f
 =e^{tA}f+\sum_{r=1}^{m-1}h^{2r}\mathcal E_r(t)f
 +h^{2m}\mathcal R_{m,h}(t)f,
\]
and there exist constants $C_m,\Omega_m\ge0$ such that, for
$0<h\le h_0$ and $t\ge0$,
\[
 \|\mathcal R_{m,h}(t)f\|_{L^2}
 \le C_m e^{\Omega_m t}t
 \|f\|_{H^{(p+2)m}}.
\]
Consequently, with $E_{2r}(t):=t^{2r}\mathcal E_r(t)$ and $h=t/N$,
\[
 \|R^{\mathrm{prod}}_{m,N}(t)f\|_{L^2}
 \le C_m e^{\Omega_m t}
 \frac{t^{2m+1}}{N^{2m}}
 \|f\|_{H^{(p+2)m}},
 \qquad 0\le t\le h_0N.
\]
Thus the centered-difference products satisfy the pointwise expansion
\eqref{eq:product-expansion} through depth $m$ on $H^{(p+2)m}$.
<!-- end lemma-4 -->
<!-- proof-10: body_md -->
Put $\varepsilon=h^2$, $U_h(t):=e^{tA_h}$, and $T(t):=e^{tA}$.
On smooth data, the Duhamel identity
\begin{equation}
 U_h(t)
 =T(t)+\int_0^t U_h(t-s)(A_h-A)T(s)\,ds
 \label{eq:duhamel-identity-group}
\end{equation}
holds; the estimates below extend to the Sobolev spaces by density.  We iterate \eqref{eq:duhamel-identity-group} finitely many times and make the expansion in $\varepsilon$ at $0$.

Suppose that a branch has already been formed by lower defects
$R_{r_1},\ldots,R_{r_j}$ with accumulated index
\[
 q:=r_1+\cdots+r_j<m.
\]
At the next Duhamel step we use the finite generator expansion at the
\emph{remaining depth} $m-q$:
\begin{equation}
 A_h-A
 =\sum_{r=1}^{m-q-1}\varepsilon^rR_r
  +\varepsilon^{m-q}Q_{m-q}(h).
 \label{eq:remaining-depth-expansion}
\end{equation}
Thus the branch either chooses another $R_r$, in which case its
accumulated index remains strictly below $m$, or it chooses the
terminal factor $Q_{m-q}(h)$ and stops.  Since each selected defect
index is at least one, this procedure ends after at most $m$
Duhamel steps.  In particular, the original generator remainder
$\varepsilon^mQ_m(h)$ occurs only in the length-one terminal branch;
if lower defects have already been selected, the terminal factor is the
lower-depth remainder $Q_{m-q}(h)$ from \eqref{eq:remaining-depth-expansion}.

Collecting all branches of total index $r<m$ gives the coefficient
$\mathcal E_r(t)$.  It is a finite sum of complete words
\[
 \int_{0<s_1<\cdots<s_\ell<t}
 T(t-s_\ell)R_{r_\ell}
 T(s_\ell-s_{\ell-1})\cdots
 R_{r_1}T(s_1)\,ds_1\cdots ds_\ell,
 \qquad r_1+\cdots+r_\ell=r.
\]
If $d_j:=2r_j+p$, then, reading the word from right to left and using
the group bound at each intermediate Sobolev index,
\[
 \left\|\int_{0<s_1<\cdots<s_\ell<t}\text{word}\,ds_1\cdots ds_\ell\,f\right\|_{L^2}
 \le C_{r,\ell}e^{\Omega_r t}\frac{t^\ell}{\ell!}
 \|f\|_{H^{d_1+\cdots+d_\ell}},
\]
while
\[
 d_1+\cdots+d_\ell
 =2r+p\ell\le(p+2)r,
\]
because $\ell\le r$.  Hence every coefficient of index $r<m$ is
controlled on $H^{(p+2)m}$.

It remains to estimate the terminal branches.  A terminal word of
length $\ell$ has lower indices
$r_1,\ldots,r_{\ell-1}$ with
\[
 q:=r_1+\cdots+r_{\ell-1}<m
\]
and one terminal factor $Q_{m-q}(h)$.  Up to a rearrangement of the lower defects, it has the form
\begin{align*}
 \int_{0<s_1<\cdots<s_\ell<t}
 &U_h(t-s_\ell)Q_{m-q}(h)
 T(s_\ell-s_{\ell-1})R_{r_{\ell-1}}\cdots\\
 &\hspace{35mm}\cdots
 T(s_2-s_1)R_{r_1}T(s_1)f\,
 ds_1\cdots ds_\ell .
\end{align*}
The total Sobolev loss in such a word is
\[
 \sum_{j=1}^{\ell-1}(2r_j+p)
 +(2(m-q)+p)
 =2m+p\ell
 \le(p+2)m,
\]
since $\ell\le m$.  The leftmost factor $U_h(t-s_\ell)$ is used only
on $L^2$, where it is uniformly stable.  The remaining semigroup
factors are controlled by the Sobolev group bounds for $T$.  Therefore
all terminal words of length $\ell$ satisfy
\[
 \|\text{terminal word}\|_{L^2}
 \le C_m e^{\omega_m t}\frac{t^\ell}{\ell!}
 \|f\|_{H^{(p+2)m}}.
\]
Every terminal branch is a coefficient of the common factor
$\varepsilon^m=h^{2m}$.  After this factor is removed in the definition
of $\mathcal R_{m,h}(t)$ and the finitely many branches are summed,
\[
 \|\mathcal R_{m,h}(t)f\|_{L^2}
 \le C_m e^{\omega_m t}
 \sum_{\ell=1}^m\frac{t^\ell}{\ell!}
 \|f\|_{H^{(p+2)m}}.
\]
Using
\[
 \sum_{\ell=1}^m\frac{t^\ell}{\ell!}
 \le e^t-1\le te^t,
\]
we obtain
\[
 \|\mathcal R_{m,h}(t)f\|_{L^2}
 \le C_m e^{(\omega_m+1)t}t
 \|f\|_{H^{(p+2)m}}.
\]
Letting $\Omega_m:=\omega_m+1$ proves the stated bound.
<!-- end proof-10 -->
<!-- lemma-5: body_md -->
\label{lem:parabolic-fixed-depth}
Let $A$ generate an analytic semigroup and 
                                                                      
 
choose $\omega$ sufficiently large so that
\[
B_\omega:=\omega I-A
\]
is an invertible sectorial operator.  For $\alpha\ge0$ define
\[
X_\alpha:=D(B_\omega^\alpha),
\qquad
\|u\|_{X_\alpha}:=\|B_\omega^\alpha u\|_X.
\]
 
Suppose
that, through depth $m$,
\[
 R_r:X_{\alpha+r+1}\longrightarrow X_\alpha,
\]
and                                                        
\[
 \|e^{tA}\|_{X_\alpha\to X_{\alpha+\sigma}}
 \le C_{\alpha,\sigma}e^{\Omega t}t^{-\sigma},
 \qquad t>0,\qquad 0\le\sigma\le1.
\]
Assume moreover that the centered-difference expansion through depth
$m$ is uniform for $0<h\le h_0$. Then for every
$0<\varepsilon\le1$ there are constants
$C_{m,\varepsilon},\Omega_{m,\varepsilon}\ge0$ such that, whenever
$0<t\le h_0n$,
\[
 \|\mathcal M_{2m,n}(t)f-e^{tA}f\|_{X_0}
 \le C_{m,\varepsilon}e^{\Omega_{m,\varepsilon}t}
 \frac{t^{2m+\varepsilon}}{n^{2m}}
 \|f\|_{X_{m+\varepsilon}}.
\]
In particular, at $\varepsilon=1$,
\[
 \|\mathcal M_{2m,n}(t)f-e^{tA}f\|_{X_0}
 \le C_m e^{\Omega_m t}
 \frac{t^{2m+1}}{n^{2m}}
 \|f\|_{X_{m+1}}.
\]
<!-- end lemma-5 -->
<!-- remark-4: body_md -->
This definition is independent, up to equivalence of norms, of the
particular admissible shift $\omega$.  Indeed, if $\omega_1,\omega_2$
are two such shifts and $B_j=\omega_jI-A$, then
\[
B_2
=
\bigl(I+(\omega_2-\omega_1)B_1^{-1}\bigr)B_1.
\]
The bounded operator
\[
C:=I+(\omega_2-\omega_1)B_1^{-1}
\]
is invertible and commutes with $B_1$.  Hence the sectorial functional
calculus gives
\[
B_2^\alpha=C^\alpha B_1^\alpha,
\]
with $C^{\pm\alpha}\in\mathcal L(X)$.  Consequently
\[
D(B_1^\alpha)=D(B_2^\alpha)
\]
and
\[
\|B_1^\alpha u\|_X\asymp\|B_2^\alpha u\|_X. 
\]
<!-- end remark-4 -->
<!-- proof-11: body_md -->
Consider a complete word with
$r_1+\cdots+r_\ell=r\le m$.  The defects consume the total scale amount
\[
 \sum_{j=1}^{\ell}(r_j+1)=r+\ell.
\]
Set $\theta_j=\varepsilon/\ell$ and let the semigroup interval
immediately preceding the $j$th defect supply smoothing
$1-\theta_j$.  The total smoothing supplied in this way is
$\ell-\varepsilon$, so the remaining regularity required from the datum
is exactly $r+\varepsilon$, which is bounded by $m+\varepsilon$.

Writing $\tau_j=s_j-s_{j-1}$, $s_0=0$, the norm of the resulting
time-ordered integrand is bounded by
\[
 C_{m,\varepsilon}e^{\Omega t}
 \prod_{j=1}^{\ell}\tau_j^{-(1-\theta_j)}
 \|f\|_{X_{m+\varepsilon}}.
\]
Every exponent $1-\theta_j$ is strictly smaller than one. By the Dirichlet scaling formula, for every $\ell\ge1$ and every
$a_1,\dots,a_\ell\in[0,1)$,
\begin{equation}
 \int_{0<s_1<\cdots<s_\ell<t}
 \prod_{j=1}^\ell(s_j-s_{j-1})^{-a_j}\,ds_1\cdots ds_\ell
 =
 \mathcal B(a_1,\dots,a_\ell)\,t^{\,\ell-\sum_{j=1}^\ell a_j},
\label{eq:dirichlet-simplex}
\end{equation}
where the constant
\[
 \mathcal B(a_1,\dots,a_\ell)
 :=
 \int_{0<u_1<\cdots<u_\ell<1}
 \prod_{j=1}^\ell(u_j-u_{j-1})^{-a_j}\,du_1\cdots du_\ell
\]
is finite whenever $a_1,\dots,a_\ell\in[0,1)$; here $s_0:=0$ and, in the definition of $\mathcal B$, $u_0:=0$.
Applying \eqref{eq:dirichlet-simplex} with $a_j=1-\varepsilon/\ell$ for
$j=1,\dots,\ell$, so that $\sum_{j=1}^\ell a_j=\ell-\varepsilon$,
the corresponding time-simplex integral is bounded by
\[
 C_{m,\varepsilon}e^{\Omega_{m,\varepsilon}t}\,t^{\varepsilon}.
\]
Multiplication by $h^{2m}=(t/n)^{2m}$ gives the displayed
$t^{2m+\varepsilon}n^{-2m}$ remainder. The same scale allocation
applies to the terminal Duhamel branches of total Richardson index
$m$. Summing the finitely many words proves the claim. For
$\varepsilon=1$, take $\theta_j=1/\ell$; then the simplex contributes
one full factor $t$.
<!-- end proof-11 -->
<!-- proposition-5: body_md -->
\label{prop:parabolic-sectoriality}
    
   
             
       
               
   
                                                               
                       
   
  
  
                               
   
      
   
            
       
                        
   
Assume
$
a,\varphi\in W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb R), p_0\in L^{\infty}(\mathbb R)
$
are real valued 
and set
\begin{equation}
\omega_0
:=
\operatorname*{ess\,sup}_{x\in\mathbb R}p_0(x).
\label{eq:parabolic-growth-bound}
\end{equation}
Then
\begin{equation}
\Re\langle Au,u\rangle
\le
\omega_0\|u\|_2^2,
\qquad u\in H^2(\mathbb R).
\label{eq:parabolic-quasi-dissipative}
\end{equation}
 
Moreover, for every
$
\omega>\omega_0,
$
the shifted operator
$
B_\omega:=\omega I-A
$
is an invertible sectorial operator.  More precisely, if
$
\delta_\omega:=\omega-\omega_0>0,
$
then its numerical range is contained in the sector
\[
\overline{\mathscr{S}_{\theta_\omega}}
:=
\{z\in\mathbb C:\ |\arg z|\le\theta_\omega\},
\]
where              
\begin{equation}
\tan\theta_\omega
=
\frac{\|\varphi\|_\infty}
{2\sqrt{a_0\delta_\omega}},
\qquad
0\le\theta_\omega<\frac{\pi}{2}.
\label{eq:explicit-sectorial-angle}
\end{equation}
In particular,
\[
0\in\rho(B_\omega),
\qquad
\|B_\omega^{-1}\|_{L^2\to L^2}
\le
\frac1{\omega-\omega_0}.
\]

\noindent
Consequently $A$ generates an analytic semigroup on $L^2(\mathbb R)$.
                
If
\begin{equation}
\omega-\omega_0
>
\frac{\|\varphi\|_\infty^2}{4a_0},
\label{eq:pi-four-sector-shift}
\end{equation}
then 
$
\theta_\omega<\frac{\pi}{4}.
$
<!-- end proposition-5 -->
<!-- proof-12: body_md -->
Since $D^*=-D$ on $L^2(\mathbb R)$ and $a$ is real-valued,
\[
\langle DaDu,u\rangle
=
-\langle aDu,Du\rangle
=
-\int_\mathbb R a(x)|Du(x)|^2\,dx.
\]
Moreover, because $\varphi$ is real-valued,
$
B:=\frac12(\varphi D+D\varphi)
$
is skew-symmetric on $H^1(\mathbb R)$ and hence
\[
\Re\langle B u,u\rangle=0.
\]
Therefore
\begin{align*}
\Re\langle Au,u\rangle
&=
-\int_\mathbb R a(x)|Du(x)|^2\,dx
+
\int_\mathbb R p_0(x)|u(x)|^2\,dx
\\
&\le
\omega_0\|u\|_2^2,
\end{align*}
which proves \eqref{eq:parabolic-quasi-dissipative}.

Next, we prove sectoriality.  Fix
$
\omega>\omega_0
$
and set
$
\delta_\omega:=\omega-\omega_0>0.
$ 
Consider on $H^1(\mathbb R)$ the sesquilinear form associated to $
B_\omega=\omega I-A.
$:
\begin{align}
\mathfrak b_\omega[u,v]
&:=
\int_\mathbb R a\,Du\,\overline{Dv}\,dx
+
\int_\mathbb R(\omega-p_0)u\overline v\,dx
\nonumber\\
&\quad
-\frac12\int_\mathbb R\varphi\,Du\,\overline v\,dx
+
\frac12\int_\mathbb R\varphi\,u\,\overline{Dv}\,dx.
\label{eq:parabolic-sectorial-form}
\end{align}
                                 
  
                     
  
$\mathfrak b_\omega$ is continuous on $H^1(\mathbb R)$ since
$a,\varphi,p_0\in L^\infty(\mathbb R)$.
 
For $u\in H^1(\mathbb R)$, the last two terms in
\eqref{eq:parabolic-sectorial-form} sum up to a purely imaginary, and hence
\begin{align}
\Re\mathfrak b_\omega[u,u]
&=
\int_\mathbb R a|Du|^2\,dx
+
\int_\mathbb R(\omega-p_0)|u|^2\,dx
\nonumber\\
&\ge
a_0\|Du\|_{L^2}^2
+
\delta_\omega\|u\|_{L^2}^2,
\label{eq:parabolic-form-coercive}
\end{align}
i.e. $\mathfrak b_\omega$ is coercive on $H^1(\mathbb R)$.
 
Moreover,
\begin{align*}
\left|
\Im\mathfrak b_\omega[u,u]
\right|
&=
\left|
\Im
\int_\mathbb R\varphi\,Du\,\overline u\,dx
\right|
\\
&\le
\|\varphi\|_\infty
\|Du\|_{L^2}\|u\|_{L^2}.
\end{align*}
Using
\[
2\sqrt{a_0\delta_\omega}
\|Du\|_{L^2}\|u\|_{L^2}
\le
a_0\|Du\|_2^2
+
\delta_\omega\|u\|_{L^2}^2,
\]
we obtain
\begin{align}
\left|
\Im\mathfrak b_\omega[u,u]
\right|
&\le
\frac{\|\varphi\|_\infty}
{2\sqrt{a_0\delta_\omega}}
\left(
a_0\|Du\|_{L^2}^2
+
\delta_\omega\|u\|_{L^2}^2
\right)
\nonumber\\
&\le
\frac{\|\varphi\|_\infty}
{2\sqrt{a_0\delta_\omega}}
\Re\mathfrak b_\omega[u,u].
\label{eq:parabolic-form-sector}
\end{align}
Thus $\mathfrak b_\omega$ is a densely defined closed sectorial form
with semi-angle $\theta_\omega$ satisfying
\[
\tan\theta_\omega
\le
\frac{\|\varphi\|_\infty}
{2\sqrt{a_0(\omega-\omega_0)}}.
\]
 
{            
By the first representation theorem for closed sectorial forms
(see, e.g., \cite[Chapter~VI, \S2]{Kato}), 
                               
                                                             
 $B_\omega$, which is the operator associated to the closed sectorial form $\mathfrak b_\omega$, is a $m$-sectorial operator. 
Since its semi-angle is strictly smaller than $\pi/2$,
$-B_\omega$ generates a bounded holomorphic semigroup.
}
 
                                                          
                                                          
                                                                   
                                                                      
               
   
                             
   
    
   
                  
  
           
  
          
   
                                                 
                       
 
Furthermore, \eqref{eq:parabolic-form-coercive} implies
\[
\Re\mathfrak b_\omega[u,u]
\ge
\delta_\omega\|u\|_2^2.
\]
Then, Lax--Milgram theorem                                                                        
implies
$
0\in\rho(B_\omega)
$
and
\[
\|B_\omega^{-1}\|_{L^2\to L^2}
\le
\delta_\omega^{-1}
=
\frac1{\omega-\omega_0}.
\]
 
Finally, $B_\omega$ sectorial with angle strictly smaller than
$\pi/2$ implies that
\[
A=\omega I-B_\omega
\]
generates an analytic $C_0$-semigroup on $L^2(\mathbb R)$.

If
\[
\omega-\omega_0
>
\frac{\|\varphi\|_\infty^2}{4a_0},
\]
then
\[
\frac{\|\varphi\|_\infty}
{2\sqrt{a_0(\omega-\omega_0)}}<1,
\]
and therefore $\theta_\omega<\pi/4$.
<!-- end proof-12 -->
<!-- proposition-6: body_md -->
\label{prop:parabolic-analyticity}
Let
\[
A u
=
a(x)u''+b(x)u'+c(x)u,
\qquad
D(A)=H^2(\mathbb R),
\]
acting on \(L^2(\mathbb R)\). Assume that
\[
a(x)\ge a_0>0,
\]
and that the coefficients have sufficiently many bounded derivatives
for the finite Sobolev range considered below. In particular,
\[
a\in W^{1,\infty}(\mathbb R),
\qquad
b,c\in L^\infty(\mathbb R).
\]
Then there exists \(\omega\in\mathbb R\) sufficiently large such that
\[
L:=\omega-A
\]
is invertible and sectorial of angle strictly smaller than \(\pi/2\).
Consequently \(A\) generates an analytic \(C_0\)-semigroup on
\(L^2(\mathbb R)\) satisfying
\[
\|e^{tA}\|_{\mathcal L(L^2)}
\le
M e^{\omega t},
\qquad t\ge0.
\]

Moreover, if
\[
X_\beta:=D(L^\beta),
\qquad
\|u\|_{X_\beta}:=\|L^\beta u\|_{L^2},
\]
then, throughout every bounded range of \(\beta\) allowed by the
regularity of the coefficients,
\[
X_\beta\simeq H^{2\beta}(\mathbb R),
\]
with equivalence of norms. In particular, for \(\alpha,\sigma\ge0\)
in this range,
\[
\|e^{tA}\|_{X_\alpha\to X_{\alpha+\sigma}}
\le
C_{\alpha,\sigma}e^{\omega t}t^{-\sigma},
\qquad t>0,
\]
or equivalently,
\[
\|e^{tA}\|_{H^{2\alpha}\to H^{2\alpha+2\sigma}}
\le
C_{\alpha,\sigma}e^{\omega t}t^{-\sigma}.
\]
<!-- end proposition-6 -->
<!-- proof-13: body_md -->
We divide the proof into three steps.

\medskip
\noindent
\textbf{Step 1: sectoriality and analytic-semigroup generation.}

For \(\omega\in\mathbb R\), define on
\[
V:=H^1(\mathbb R)
\]
the sesquilinear form
\begin{equation}
\mathfrak a_\omega(u,v)
:=
\int_{\mathbb R}a\,u'\overline{v'}\,dx
+
\int_{\mathbb R}(a'-b)u'\overline v\,dx
+
\int_{\mathbb R}(\omega-c)u\overline v\,dx.
\label{eq:parabolic-sectorial-form}
\end{equation}
For \(u\in H^2(\mathbb R)\), integration by parts gives
\[
\begin{aligned}
\mathfrak a_\omega(u,v)
&=
-\int_{\mathbb R}(a u')'\overline v\,dx
+
\int_{\mathbb R}(a'-b)u'\overline v\,dx
+
\int_{\mathbb R}(\omega-c)u\overline v\,dx
\\
&=
\int_{\mathbb R}
\bigl(-a u''-b u'+(\omega-c)u\bigr)\overline v\,dx
\\
&=
\langle (\omega-A)u,v\rangle_{L^2}.
\end{aligned}
\]
Thus \(\mathfrak a_\omega\) is the form naturally associated with
\(L=\omega-A\).

By uniform ellipticity,
\[
a(x)\ge a_0>0.
\]
Hence
\[
\begin{aligned}
\operatorname{Re}\mathfrak a_\omega(u,u)
&\ge
a_0\|u'\|_2^2
-
\|a'-b\|_\infty
\|u'\|_2\|u\|_2
\\
&\qquad
+
\bigl(\omega-\|\operatorname{Re}c\|_\infty\bigr)
\|u\|_2^2.
\end{aligned}
\]
Young's inequality yields
\[
\|a'-b\|_\infty\|u'\|_2\|u\|_2
\le
\frac{a_0}{2}\|u'\|_2^2
+
\frac{\|a'-b\|_\infty^2}{2a_0}\|u\|_2^2.
\]
Therefore
\begin{equation}
\operatorname{Re}\mathfrak a_\omega(u,u)
\ge
\frac{a_0}{2}\|u'\|_2^2
+
\left(
\omega-\|\operatorname{Re}c\|_\infty
-\frac{\|a'-b\|_\infty^2}{2a_0}
\right)
\|u\|_2^2.
\label{eq:parabolic-coercivity-estimate}
\end{equation}
Choosing
\[
\omega>
\|\operatorname{Re}c\|_\infty
+
\frac{\|a'-b\|_\infty^2}{2a_0},
\]
we obtain, for some \(\kappa>0\),
\begin{equation}
\operatorname{Re}\mathfrak a_\omega(u,u)
\ge
\kappa\|u\|_{H^1}^2.
\label{eq:parabolic-coercive-form}
\end{equation}

On the other hand,
\begin{equation}
|\mathfrak a_\omega(u,v)|
\le
C_\omega
\|u\|_{H^1}\|v\|_{H^1}.
\label{eq:parabolic-form-boundedness}
\end{equation}
Thus \(\mathfrak a_\omega\) is densely defined, closed and sectorial
on \(L^2(\mathbb R)\). By the first representation theorem for
sectorial forms, the associated operator is \(m\)-sectorial.

We next identify that operator. If \(u\) belongs to its operator
domain, then \(u\in H^1(\mathbb R)\) and
\[
Lu
=
-a u''-b u'+(\omega-c)u
\in L^2(\mathbb R)
\]
in the distributional sense. Hence
\[
a u''
=
-Lu-bu'+(\omega-c)u.
\]
The right-hand side belongs to \(L^2(\mathbb R)\), and
\(a^{-1}\in L^\infty(\mathbb R)\) by uniform ellipticity. Therefore
\[
u''\in L^2(\mathbb R),
\]
and consequently
\[
u\in H^2(\mathbb R).
\]
Conversely, every \(u\in H^2(\mathbb R)\) belongs to the form
operator domain by the integration-by-parts identity above. Thus
\begin{equation}
D(L)=H^2(\mathbb R),
\qquad
L=\omega-A.
\label{eq:parabolic-domain-identification}
\end{equation}

Since \(L\) is \(m\)-sectorial of angle strictly smaller than
\(\pi/2\), \(-L\) generates a bounded analytic \(C_0\)-semigroup.
Because
\[
A=\omega-L,
\]
we have
\[
e^{tA}=e^{\omega t}e^{-tL},
\]
and therefore
\begin{equation}
\|e^{tA}\|
\le
M e^{\omega t}.
\label{eq:parabolic-semigroup-growth}
\end{equation}
This is the precise sense in which uniform ellipticity, together with
the bounded lower-order coefficients, yields analytic-semigroup
generation: uniform ellipticity provides coercivity of the principal
part, sectorial-form theory yields the sectorial realization, and
sectoriality yields analyticity.

\medskip
\noindent
\textbf{Step 2: analytic regularization.}

Since \(-L\) generates a bounded analytic semigroup, the sectorial
functional calculus gives, for every \(\sigma\ge0\),
\begin{equation}
\|L^\sigma e^{-tL}\|_{\mathcal L(L^2)}
\le
C_\sigma t^{-\sigma},
\qquad t>0.
\label{eq:sectorial-smoothing}
\end{equation}
For instance, this follows from the Dunford representation
\[
L^\sigma e^{-tL}
=
\frac{1}{2\pi i}
\int_\Gamma
z^\sigma e^{-tz}(z-L)^{-1}\,dz,
\]
where \(\Gamma\) is a sectorial contour. Along \(\Gamma\),
\[
\|(z-L)^{-1}\|
\le
\frac{C}{|z|}
\]
and
\[
|e^{-tz}|
\le
e^{-c t|z|}.
\]
Hence
\[
\begin{aligned}
\|L^\sigma e^{-tL}\|
&\le
C\int_0^\infty
r^{\sigma-1}e^{-ctr}\,dr
\\
&=
C t^{-\sigma}
\int_0^\infty
s^{\sigma-1}e^{-cs}\,ds
\\
&\le
C_\sigma t^{-\sigma}.
\end{aligned}
\]

Define
\[
X_\alpha:=D(L^\alpha),
\qquad
\|u\|_{X_\alpha}:=\|L^\alpha u\|_2.
\]
Since \(0\in\rho(L)\), this is equivalent to the usual graph norm on
\(D(L^\alpha)\).

If \(u\in X_\alpha\), then the functional calculus gives the
commutation relation
\[
L^\alpha e^{-tL}u
=
e^{-tL}L^\alpha u.
\]
Consequently,
\[
\begin{aligned}
\|e^{tA}u\|_{X_{\alpha+\sigma}}
&=
\|L^{\alpha+\sigma}e^{tA}u\|_2
\\
&=
e^{\omega t}
\|L^{\alpha+\sigma}e^{-tL}u\|_2
\\
&=
e^{\omega t}
\|L^\sigma e^{-tL}L^\alpha u\|_2
\\
&\le
C_{\alpha,\sigma}
e^{\omega t}
t^{-\sigma}
\|u\|_{X_\alpha}.
\end{aligned}
\]
Thus
\begin{equation}
\boxed{
\|e^{tA}\|_{X_\alpha\to X_{\alpha+\sigma}}
\le
C_{\alpha,\sigma}
e^{\omega t}t^{-\sigma}.
}
\label{eq:fractional-scale-smoothing}
\end{equation}

\medskip
\noindent
\textbf{Step 3: identification of the fractional-domain scale.}

We first identify the integer powers of \(L\).
Uniform ellipticity gives the standard elliptic regularity estimate
\begin{equation}
\|u\|_{H^{s+2}}
\le
C_s
\bigl(
\|Lu\|_{H^s}+\|u\|_2
\bigr)
\label{eq:elliptic-regularity}
\end{equation}
for every integer \(s\ge0\) in the finite range permitted by the
regularity of the coefficients.

For completeness, when \(s=0\), the equation
\[
Lu=-a u''-b u'+(\omega-c)u
\]
gives
\[
a u''
=
-Lu-bu'+(\omega-c)u.
\]
Using the coercive estimate from Step~1 to control \(\|u'\|_2\),
together with \(a^{-1},b,c\in L^\infty\), yields
\[
\|u\|_{H^2}
\le
C\bigl(
\|Lu\|_2+\|u\|_2
\bigr).
\]
For higher integer \(s\), differentiating the equation \(Lu=f\)
gives
\[
D^s(au'')
=
aD^{s+2}u
+
\sum_{j=1}^{s}
\binom{s}{j}
D^ja\,D^{s-j+2}u.
\]
The first term contains the only derivative of \(u\) of order
\(s+2\); all other terms contain derivatives of order at most
\(s+1\). Since \(a\ge a_0>0\), one can solve for the highest
derivative and close the estimate inductively, obtaining
\eqref{eq:elliptic-regularity}.

Because \(0\in\rho(L)\),
\[
\|u\|_2
\le
\|L^{-1}\|\,\|Lu\|_2,
\]
so \eqref{eq:elliptic-regularity} improves to
\begin{equation}
\|u\|_{H^{s+2}}
\le
C_s\|Lu\|_{H^s}.
\label{eq:elliptic-resolvent-estimate}
\end{equation}
Conversely, the differential expression itself gives
\begin{equation}
\|Lu\|_{H^s}
\le
C_s\|u\|_{H^{s+2}}.
\label{eq:elliptic-expression-estimate}
\end{equation}
Hence
\[
L:H^{s+2}(\mathbb R)\longrightarrow H^s(\mathbb R)
\]
has the expected elliptic order two, with equivalence of the
corresponding graph and Sobolev norms.

We now prove inductively that
\begin{equation}
D(L^k)=H^{2k}(\mathbb R).
\label{eq:integer-power-domain}
\end{equation}
The case \(k=1\) follows from \eqref{eq:parabolic-domain-identification}. Suppose
\[
D(L^k)=H^{2k}(\mathbb R).
\]
Then
\[
u\in D(L^{k+1})
\]
if and only if
\[
u\in D(L)
\qquad\text{and}\qquad
Lu\in D(L^k).
\]
By the induction hypothesis,
\[
Lu\in H^{2k}(\mathbb R).
\]
Applying \eqref{eq:elliptic-resolvent-estimate} with \(s=2k\) gives
\[
u\in H^{2k+2}(\mathbb R).
\]
Thus
\[
D(L^{k+1})\subset H^{2k+2}(\mathbb R).
\]
Conversely, if
\[
u\in H^{2k+2}(\mathbb R),
\]
then \eqref{eq:elliptic-expression-estimate} implies
\[
Lu\in H^{2k}(\mathbb R)=D(L^k),
\]
and therefore
\[
u\in D(L^{k+1}).
\]
Hence
\[
D(L^{k+1})=H^{2k+2}(\mathbb R).
\]
Moreover,
\begin{equation}
\|L^k u\|_2
\simeq
\|u\|_{H^{2k}}.
\label{eq:integer-power-graph-norm}
\end{equation}

It remains to pass from integer powers to fractional powers.
For the present uniformly elliptic realization, the standard
complex-power theorem for elliptic operators gives
\begin{equation}
D(L^{k+\theta})
=
[D(L^k),D(L^{k+1})]_\theta,
\qquad
0<\theta<1,
\label{eq:fractional-domain-interpolation}
\end{equation}
in every bounded range in which the coefficient regularity permits
the preceding elliptic estimates. Equivalently, one may use the
bounded-imaginary-power theorem for uniformly elliptic operators.
See, for example, the classical complex-power theory of Seeley
together with the interpolation theory of sectorial operators.

Using \eqref{eq:integer-power-domain},
\[
D(L^{k+\theta})
=
[H^{2k}(\mathbb R),H^{2k+2}(\mathbb R)]_\theta.
\]
The Bessel-potential Sobolev scale satisfies
\[
[H^{s_0}(\mathbb R),H^{s_1}(\mathbb R)]_\theta
=
H^{(1-\theta)s_0+\theta s_1}(\mathbb R),
\]
and therefore
\[
\begin{aligned}
D(L^{k+\theta})
&=
H^{(1-\theta)2k+\theta(2k+2)}(\mathbb R)
\\
&=
H^{2k+2\theta}(\mathbb R)
\\
&=
H^{2(k+\theta)}(\mathbb R).
\end{aligned}
\]
Thus, writing \(\beta=k+\theta\),
\begin{equation}
\boxed{
X_\beta
=
D(L^\beta)
\simeq
H^{2\beta}(\mathbb R).
}
\label{eq:fractional-domain-sobolev}
\end{equation}

Combining \eqref{eq:fractional-scale-smoothing} with \eqref{eq:fractional-domain-sobolev} yields the Sobolev
regularizing estimate
\begin{equation}
\boxed{
\|e^{tA}\|_{H^{2\alpha}\to H^{2\alpha+2\sigma}}
\le
C_{\alpha,\sigma}
e^{\omega t}
t^{-\sigma},
\qquad t>0.
}
\label{eq:sobolev-smoothing}
\end{equation}
This proves the result.
<!-- end proof-13 -->
<!-- corollary-1: body_md -->
\label{cor:parabolic-richardson}
Assume $a\ge a_0>0$ and
\[
 a\in W^{2m+4,\infty}(\mathbb R),\qquad b\in W^{2m+3,\infty}(\mathbb R),\qquad c\in W^{2m+2,\infty}(\mathbb R).
\]
                         
Then 
                                             
$A$ generates an analytic semigroup. For every $\delta>0$, set
$\eta_\delta:=\min\{\delta/2,1\}$. There are constants
$C_{m,\delta},\Omega_{m,\delta}\ge0$ such that, for $0<t\le h_0n$,
\[
 \|\mathcal M_{2m,n}(t)f-e^{tA}f\|_{L^2}
 \le C_{m,\delta}e^{\Omega_{m,\delta}t}
 \frac{t^{2m+\eta_\delta}}{n^{2m}}
 \|f\|_{H^{2m+\delta}}.
\]
In particular, for $f\in H^{2m+2}$,
\[
 \|\mathcal M_{2m,n}(t)f-e^{tA}f\|_{L^2}
 \le C_m e^{\Omega_mt}
 \frac{t^{2m+1}}{n^{2m}}
 \|f\|_{H^{2m+2}}.
\]
<!-- end corollary-1 -->
<!-- corollary-2: body_md -->
\label{cor:schrodinger-richardson}
Under the above assumptions, for every\footnote{Observe that $\mathcal J H^{4m}(\mathbb R)=\mathcal J^{-1}H^{4m}(\mathbb R)=H^{4m}(\mathbb R)$.} $f\in \mathcal J^{-1}H^{4m}=H^{4m}$
and $0\le t\le h_0n$,
\[
 \|\mathcal J^{-1}\mathcal M_{2m,n}(t)\mathcal J f-e^{t A_0}f\|_{L^2}
 \le C_m e^{\Omega_m t}
 \frac{t^{2m+1}}{n^{2m}}
 \|f\|_{H^{4m}}.
\]
<!-- end corollary-2 -->
<!-- corollary-3: body_md -->
\label{cor:dispersive-richardson}
Under the above assumptions, if\footnote{Observe that $\mathcal J H^{5m}(\mathbb R)=\mathcal J^{-1}H^{5m}(\mathbb R)=H^{5m}(\mathbb R)$.} $f\in \mathcal J^{-1}H^{5m}(\mathbb R)=H^{5m}(\mathbb R)$ and
$0\le t\le h_0n$, then
\[
 \|\mathcal J^{-1}\mathcal M_{2m,n}(t)\mathcal J f-e^{tA_0}f\|_{L^2}
 \le C_m e^{\Omega_m t}
 \frac{t^{2m+1}}{n^{2m}}
 \|f\|_{H^{5m}}.
\]
<!-- end corollary-3 -->
<!-- remark-5: body_md -->
\label{rem:remove-quadratic-derivative}
The absence of a $D^2$ term is not restrictive.
Indeed, for
\[
 \widehat A=aD^3+\beta D^2+\gamma D+\delta,\qquad a(x)\ne0,
\]
choose a nonvanishing multiplier $\mu$ with
\[
 \frac{\mu'}{\mu}=-\frac{\beta}{3a}.
\]
Since $\mu^{-1}D\mu=D+\mu'/\mu$, the coefficient of $D^2$ in
$\mu^{-1}\widehat A\mu$ is $3a\,\mu'/\mu+\beta=0$. Hence
\[
 \mu^{-1}\widehat A\mu=aD^3+bD+c
\]
for suitable lower-order coefficients $b,c$. Globally, this conjugation
is an equivalence on the function spaces used below whenever $\mu$ and
$\mu^{-1}$ are bounded multipliers there; for example, this holds if
                                                    
                                                     
$\beta$ and $a$ are uniformly bounded away from both $0$ and $\infty$, and $\beta/a\in L^1(\mathbb R)$.
<!-- end remark-5 -->
<!-- remark-6: body_md -->
\label{rem:nonlinear-parents}
The three linear model classes above arise naturally as linearization 
(variational or tangent equations) of nonlinear PDEs.

\emph{Parabolic case.}
Consider the scalar quasilinear convection--diffusion--reaction equation
\[
 u_t=\partial_x\!\bigl(d(u)u_x\bigr)-\partial_xF(u)+R(u),
 \qquad d(u)>0.
\]
Let $\xi=x-st$ and let $U(\xi)$ be a travelling-wave profile.  Writing
\[
 u(t,x)=U(\xi)+\varepsilon v(t,\xi)+O(\varepsilon^2)
\]
and retaining the terms linear in $\varepsilon$ gives
\begin{align*}
 v_t={}&d(U)v_{\xi\xi}
 +\bigl(2d'(U)U'+s-F'(U)\bigr)v_\xi\\
 &+\bigl(d''(U)(U')^2+d'(U)U''-F''(U)U'+R'(U)\bigr)v.
\end{align*}
Thus the tangent equation has exactly the form
$a(\xi)D^2+b(\xi)D+c(\xi)$.  For viscous Burgers,
$d\equiv\nu$, $F(u)=u^2/2$, and $R\equiv0$, this reduces to
\[
 v_t=\nu v_{\xi\xi}+(s-U)v_\xi-U'v.
\]
For the stationary normalization (see e.g. Guba--Lorenz
\cite[Sec.~1, Eq.~(1)]{GubaLorenz11}) $\nu=1$, $s=0$,  the corresponding Burgers
linearization as $Lv=v''-(Uv)'$.  Likewise, Fisher--KPP,
$d\equiv D$, $F\equiv0$, $R(u)=ru(1-u)$, gives
\[
 v_t=Dv_{\xi\xi}+sv_\xi+r(1-2U)v.
\]

\emph{Schr\"odinger case.}
A direct nonlinear parent of the second model is the semilinear
Schr\"odinger-type equation
\[
 u_t=ia(x)u_{xx}+b(x)u_x+c(x)u+i\gamma(x)|u|^2u.
\]
Since the Fr\'echet derivative of $|u|^2u$ at $u=0$ vanishes, its
linearization at the zero state is exactly
\[
 v_t=\bigl(ia(x)D^2+b(x)D+c(x)\bigr)v.
\]
For a nonzero coherent state the scalar equation is replaced, in
general, by a coupled Hamiltonian linearization whose entries are
second-order Schr\"odinger operators.  For example, consider the cubic focusing NLS
\[
 i\psi_t+\psi_{xx}+|\psi|^2\psi=0.
\]
For a real standing wave $\psi=e^{i\omega t}Q(x)$, the perturbation
$\psi=e^{i\omega t}(Q+r+is)$ yields at first order
\[
 \partial_t\binom r s
 =\begin{pmatrix}0&L_-\\-L_+&0\end{pmatrix}\binom r s,
 \qquad
 L_-=-D^2+\omega-Q^2,\qquad
 L_+=-D^2+\omega-3Q^2.
\]
This standard NLS/GP linearization is written explicitly, for the
corresponding potential problem and general power nonlinearity, in
Gang--Weinstein \cite[Sec.~2.3, Eqs.~(2.2)--(2.4)]{GangWeinstein11}.
Thus the scalar model treated here is the exact tangent evolution at the
zero state, while the same second-order Schr\"odinger operators occur as
blocks of the linearization about nonzero coherent states.

\emph{Third-order dispersive case.}
Consider more generally
\[
 q_t=a(x)q_{xxx}+H(x,q,q_x),
\]
and let $U(x)$ be a stationary profile.  Substituting
$q=U+\varepsilon v+O(\varepsilon^2)$ gives directly
\[
 v_t=a(x)v_{xxx}
 +H_{q_x}(x,U,U')v_x
 +H_q(x,U,U')v,
\]
which is exactly of the form $a(x)D^3+b(x)D+c(x)$.  This is the
Fr\'echet-linearized (symmetry) equation used by G\"urses--Karasu
\cite[Eq.~(2)]{GursesKarasu95}; for the autonomous class
$q_t=q_{xxx}+F(q,q_x)$ they display precisely
$\sigma_t=\sigma_{xxx}+F_{q_x}\sigma_x+F_q\sigma$ in
\cite[Eq.~(11)]{GursesKarasu95}.  As the familiar gKdV specialization,
\[
 u_t+u_{xxx}+\partial_x f(u)=0
\]
linearized in the frame $\xi=x-st$ about a travelling profile $U$ gives
\[
 v_t=-v_{\xi\xi\xi}
 +\bigl(s-f'(U)\bigr)v_\xi
 -f''(U)U'v.
\]
For KdV, $f(u)=3u^2$, hence
$b=s-6U$ and $c=-6U'$.  The sign of the cubic principal part may of
course be reversed by the reflection $\xi\mapsto-\xi$.

These examples explain why the three classes are useful test cases for
the general machinery developed here: they are tangent equations for
nonlinear parabolic fronts, nonlinear Schr\"odinger dynamics, and
KdV-type dispersive waves.  The centered-difference and Richardson
arguments themselves are not tied to these nonlinear equations; they
apply whenever the relevant linearized generator can be put into one of
the structural forms treated above.
<!-- end remark-6 -->
<!-- lemma-6: body_md -->
\label{lem:analytic-multiplier}
Let $g\in \mathcal{H}_{\rho_*}$ and $0<\rho<{\rho_*}$. Then
\begin{equation}
\|gf\|_\rho
\le
\sqrt2\,\|g\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho_*}}\|f\|_\rho.
\label{eq:analytic-multiplier}
\end{equation}
Moreover, if $0<\rho<\rho_*$, Cauchy's estimate gives
\begin{equation}
\|g^{(q)}\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho}}
\le
\frac{q!}{(\rho_*-\rho)^q}\,
\|g\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho_*}},
\qquad q\in\mathbb N_0.
\label{eq:coefficient-cauchy}                                            
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-6 -->
<!-- proof-14: body_md -->
On the two boundary lines of $\mathscr S_\rho$,
\[
\|g(\cdot\pm i\rho)f(\cdot\pm i\rho)\|_{L^2}
\le
\|g\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho_*}}
\|f(\cdot\pm i\rho)\|_{L^2}.
\]
Hence
\[
\|gf\|_{\partial,\rho}
\le
\|g\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho_*}}\|f\|_{\partial,\rho},
\]
whence \eqref{eq:analytic-multiplier}  follows from
\eqref{eq:boundary-fourier-equivalence}.  The derivative bound is the
standard Cauchy estimate on nested strips.
<!-- end proof-14 -->
<!-- lemma-7: body_md -->
\label{lem:analytic-liouville}
Let $\varrho_0>0$ and assume
\[
a\in \mathcal{H}_{\varrho_0}, 
\qquad
a(\bar z)=\overline{a(z)},
\]
and
\[
a(x)\ge a_0>0,
\qquad x\in\mathbb R.
\]
                                          
   
          
   
                           
   
 
                       
   
                                         
       
                           
   
    
   
               
               
   
{}
Then
\begin{equation}
\Re a(z)\ge \frac{a_0}{2},
\qquad z\in \mathscr S_{r_a}.
\label{eq:quantitative-complex-ellipticity}
\end{equation}

For $p\in\{2,3\}$ let
\[
q_p(z):=a(z)^{-1/p},
\]
where the principal branch is taken on the right half-plane.
Then $q_p$ is bounded and holomorphic on $\mathscr S_{r_a}$ and
\begin{equation}
\Re q_p(z)\ge m_p,
\qquad
|q_p(z)|\le M_p,
\qquad z\in \mathscr S_{r_a}.
\label{eq:mp-Mp-bounds}
\end{equation}

{}
 
Define
\[
\eta_p(z)
:=
\int_0^z q_p(\zeta)\,d\zeta.
\]
Then $\eta_p$ is injective on $\mathscr S_{r_a}$ and
\begin{equation}
S_{m_pr_a}
\subset
\eta_p(S_{r_a}).
\label{eq:eta-covers-strip}
\end{equation}
Consequently
\[
\chi_p:=\eta_p^{-1}
\]
is holomorphic on $\mathscr S_{m_pr_a}$ and satisfies
\begin{equation}
\chi_p(\mathscr S_{\rho_y})
\subset
\mathscr S_{\rho_y/m_p},
\qquad
0<\rho_y<m_pr_a.
\label{eq:chi-strip-inclusion}
\end{equation}
Moreover,
\begin{equation}
\eta_p(\mathscr S_{\rho_x})
\subset
\mathscr S_{M_p\rho_x},
\qquad
0<\rho_x<r_a.
\label{eq:eta-strip-inclusion}
\end{equation}

{}
<!-- end lemma-7 -->
<!-- proof-15: body_md -->
Since $a_\infty\ge a_0$, one has
\[
r_a
=
\frac{a_0\varrho_0}{4a_\infty}
\le
\frac{\varrho_0}{4}.
\]
Cauchy's estimate on $\mathscr S_{\rho_0/2}$ yields
\[
\|a'\|_{\mathcal H_{\rho_0/2}}
\le
\frac{2a_\infty}{\varrho_0}.
\]
Hence, for $z=x+iy$ with $|y|<r_a$,
\[
|a(x+iy)-a(x)|
\le
|y|\,
\|a'\|_{\mathcal H_{\rho_0/2}}
\le
\frac{2a_\infty r_a}{\varrho_0}
=
\frac{a_0}{2}.
\]
Since $a(x)\ge a_0$ on $\mathbb R$,
\[
\Re a(z)
\ge
a_0-|a(z)-a(x)|
\ge
\frac{a_0}{2},
\]
which proves \eqref{eq:quantitative-complex-ellipticity}.

Thus $a(\mathscr S_{r_a})$ lies in the open right half-plane and has no zeros
there.  The principal branch of $a^{-1/p}$ is therefore holomorphic
on $\mathscr S_{r_a}$.  Since
\[
|a(z)|\ge \Re a(z)\ge\frac{a_0}{2},
\]
we have
\[
|q_p(z)|
=
|a(z)|^{-1/p}
\le
\left(\frac{2}{a_0}\right)^{1/p}
=
M_p.
\]

To obtain a quantitative lower bound for $\Re q_p$, write
\[
a(z)=|a(z)|e^{i\theta(z)}.
\]
Since
\[
\cos\theta(z)
=
\frac{\Re a(z)}{|a(z)|}
\ge
\frac{a_0}{2a_\infty},
\]
we have
\[
|\theta(z)|
\le
\arccos\frac{a_0}{2a_\infty}.
\]
Consequently,
\begin{align*}
\Re q_p(z)
&=
|a(z)|^{-1/p}
\cos\frac{\theta(z)}p
\\
&\ge
a_\infty^{-1/p}
\cos\left(
\frac1p
\arccos\frac{a_0}{2a_\infty}
\right)
=
m_p.
\end{align*}

For $z_1,z_2\in S_{r_a}$, convexity of the strip yields
\[
\frac{\eta_p(z_2)-\eta_p(z_1)}
     {z_2-z_1}
=
\int_0^1
q_p(z_1+\theta(z_2-z_1))\,d\theta.
\]
Therefore
\[
\Re
\frac{\eta_p(z_2)-\eta_p(z_1)}
     {z_2-z_1}
\ge m_p>0,
\]
and hence $\eta_p$ is injective. 
 
Furthermore, for $z=x+iy$,
\begin{equation}
\Im\eta_p(x+iy)
=
\int_0^y
\Re q_p(x+is)\,ds.
\label{eq:imag-eta-formula}
\end{equation}
Thus
\begin{equation}
m_p|y|
\le
|\Im\eta_p(x+iy)|
\le
M_p|y|.
\label{eq:imag-eta-two-sided}
\end{equation}
In particular,
\[
\Im\eta_p(x+ir_a)\ge m_pr_a,
\qquad
\Im\eta_p(x-ir_a)\le -m_pr_a.
\]
Moreover,
\[
\partial_x\Re\eta_p(x+iy)
=
\Re q_p(x+iy)
\ge m_p,
\]
so that
\[
\Re\eta_p(x+iy)\longrightarrow\pm\infty
\qquad
\text{as }x\longrightarrow\pm\infty,
\]
uniformly for $|y|\le r_a$.

Fix $w$ with $|\Im w|<m_pr_a$.  For $X$ sufficiently large, on
the boundary of
\[
\Omega_X
=
\{z:\ |\Re z|<X,\ |\Im z|<r_a\},
\]
the four components of
\[
\eta_p(\partial\Omega_X)-w
\]
lie respectively above, to the right of, below, and to the left of
the origin.  Hence this closed curve has winding number one about
the origin.  The argument principle gives a zero of
$\eta_p(z)-w$ in $\Omega_X$; injectivity makes it unique.  This proves
\eqref{eq:eta-covers-strip}.

If $w=\eta_p(z)$, then \eqref{eq:imag-eta-two-sided} gives
\[
|\Im z|
\le
\frac{|\Im w|}{m_p},
\]
which is \eqref{eq:chi-strip-inclusion}.  The upper estimate in
\eqref{eq:imag-eta-two-sided} gives
\eqref{eq:eta-strip-inclusion}.
<!-- end proof-15 -->
<!-- lemma-8: body_md -->
\label{lem:liouville-analytic-spaces}
Assume the hypotheses of Lemma~\ref{lem:analytic-liouville}.  Set
\[
\vartheta_2:=\frac14,
\qquad
\vartheta_3:=\frac13,
\]
and, for $p=2,3$, define
\[
(\mathcal J_pf)(y)
:=
a(\chi_p(y))^{-\vartheta_p}
f(\chi_p(y)).
\]
Then the following assertions hold. 
 
If
\begin{equation}
0<\rho_y<m_p\rho_x,
\qquad
0<\rho_x<r_a,
\label{eq:J-compatible-radii}
\end{equation}
then
\begin{equation}
\mathcal J_p:
\mathcal A_{\rho_x}
\longrightarrow
\mathcal A_{\rho_y}
\label{eq:J-map-analytic}
\end{equation}
is bounded. 
 
Conversely, if
\begin{equation}
0<M_p\rho_x<\rho_y<m_pr_a,
\label{eq:Jinv-compatible-radii}
\end{equation}
then
\begin{equation}
\mathcal J_p^{-1}:
\mathcal A_{\rho_y}
\longrightarrow
\mathcal A_{\rho_x}
\label{eq:Jinv-map-analytic}
\end{equation}
is bounded.

In particular, $\mathcal J_p$ and $\mathcal J_p^{-1}$ identify the
original and normalized analytic scales up to the radius distortion
controlled by $m_p$ and $M_p$.
<!-- end lemma-8 -->
<!-- proof-16: body_md -->
We first record an elementary trace estimate.  Let
\[
0<r<R
\]
and let $Y:\mathbb R\to[-r,r]$ be measurable.  If
$f\in\mathcal A_R$, then
\begin{equation}
\int_\mathbb R
|f(x+iY(x))|^2\,dx
\le
C_{\rm tr}(R,r)^2
\|f\|_R^2,
\label{eq:variable-graph-trace}
\end{equation}
where one may take
\[
C_{\rm tr}(R,r)^2
:=
2+\frac{4r^2}{(R-r)^2}.
\]
Indeed,
\[
f(x+iY(x))
=
f(x)
+
i\int_0^{Y(x)}f'(x+is)\,ds,
\]
so Cauchy--Schwarz gives
\begin{align*}
|f(x+iY(x))|^2
&\le
2|f(x)|^2
+
2r
\int_{-r}^r
|f'(x+is)|^2\,ds.
\end{align*}
After integration in $x$,
\[
\int_\mathbb R|f(x+iY(x))|^2\,dx
\le
2\|f\|_{L^2}^2
+
4r^2
\sup_{|s|\le r}
\|f'(\cdot+is)\|_{L^2}^2.
\]
By Plancherel,
\[
\|f'(\cdot+is)\|_{L^2}
\le
\frac1{R-r}\|f\|_R,
\qquad |s|\le r,
\]
which proves \eqref{eq:variable-graph-trace}.

We now prove the bound for $\mathcal J_p$.  Fix
$|\sigma|\le\rho_y<{m_p}\rho_x<m_p r_a$ and write
\[
\chi_p(x+i\sigma)
=
X_\sigma(x)+iY_\sigma(x).
\]
By \eqref{eq:chi-strip-inclusion},
\[
|Y_\sigma(x)|
\le
\frac{\rho_y}{m_p}
<\rho_x.
\]
Moreover,
\[
\chi_p'(w)
=
\frac1{q_p(\chi_p(w))},
\]
and therefore
\[
\frac{dX_\sigma}{dx}
=
\Re\chi_p'(x+i\sigma)
=
\frac{\Re q_p(\chi_p(x+i\sigma))}
     {|q_p(\chi_p(x+i\sigma))|^2}
\ge
\frac{m_p}{M_p^2}.
\]
Thus $X_\sigma$ is strictly increasing from $\mathbb R$ onto $\mathbb R$, and
\[
dx
\le
\frac{M_p^2}{m_p}\,dX_\sigma.
\]
Since
\[
|a(\chi_p(w))|^{-\vartheta_p}
\le
\left(\frac2{a_0}\right)^{\vartheta_p},
\]
the variable-graph estimate gives
\begin{align*}
\|
(\mathcal J_pf)(\cdot+i\sigma)
\|_2^2
&\le
\left(\frac2{a_0}\right)^{2\vartheta_p}
\frac{M_p^2}{m_p}
\times
C_{\rm tr}
\left(
\rho_x,\frac{\rho_y}{m_p}
\right)^2
\|f\|_{\rho_x}^2.
\end{align*}
Applying this at $\sigma=\pm\rho_y$ and using the equivalence between
the boundary norm and $\|\cdot\|_{\rho_y}$ proves
\eqref{eq:J-map-analytic}.

\noindent
For the inverse transformation,
\[
(\mathcal J_p^{-1}g)(x)
=
a(x)^{\vartheta_p}g(\eta_p(x)).
\]
Fix $|\sigma|\le\rho_x<\rho_y/M_p<\gamma_p r_a$ and write
\[
\eta_p(x+i\sigma)
=
U_\sigma(x)+iV_\sigma(x).
\]
By \eqref{eq:eta-strip-inclusion},
\[
|V_\sigma(x)|
\le
M_p\rho_x
<\rho_y.
\]
Moreover,
\[
\frac{dU_\sigma}{dx}
=
\Re q_p(x+i\sigma)
\ge m_p,
\]
so
\[
dx\le \frac1{m_p}\,dU_\sigma.
\]
Since
\[
|a(x+i\sigma)|^{\vartheta_p}
\le a_\infty^{\vartheta_p},
\]
another application of \eqref{eq:variable-graph-trace} gives
\begin{align*}
\|
(\mathcal J_p^{-1}g)(\cdot+i\sigma)
\|_{L^2}^2
&\le
\frac{a_\infty^{2\vartheta_p}}{m_p}
C_{\rm tr}
\left(
\rho_y,M_p\rho_x
\right)^2
\|g\|_{\rho_y}^2.
\end{align*}
Taking $\sigma=\pm\rho_x$ proves
\eqref{eq:Jinv-map-analytic}.
<!-- end proof-16 -->
<!-- lemma-9: body_md -->
\label{lem:analytic-liouville}
Let $\rho_0>0$ and assume
\[
a\in \mathcal{H}_{\rho_0}, 
\qquad
a(\bar z)=\overline{a(z)},
\]
and
\[
a(x)\ge a_0>0,
\qquad x\in\mathbb R.
\]
For $p\in\{2,3\}$ define on the real axis
\[
\eta_p(x)
:=
\int_0^x a(s)^{-1/p}\,ds .
\]
Then there exist $r_p>0$ and $\widetilde r_p>0$ such that:

\begin{enumerate}
\item the principal branch
\[
q_p(z):=a(z)^{-1/p}
\]
is bounded and holomorphic on $\mathscr S_{r_p}$;

\item there exists $m_p>0$ such that
\[
\Re q_p(z)\ge m_p,
\qquad z\in \mathscr S_{r_p};
\]

\item
\[
\eta_p(z):=\int_0^zq_p(\zeta)\,d\zeta
\]
is injective on $\mathscr S_{r_p}$;

\item
\[
S_{\widetilde r_p}\subset\eta_p(\mathscr S_{r_p}),
\qquad
\widetilde r_p:=m_pr_p,
\]
and therefore
\[
\chi_p:=\eta_p^{-1}
\]
is holomorphic on $\mathscr S_{\widetilde r_p}$ with
\[
\chi_p(\mathscr S_{\widetilde r_p})\subset \mathscr S_{r_p}.
\]
\end{enumerate}

In particular, if $F\in \mathcal{H}_{r_p}\ {}$, then
\[
F\circ\chi_p\in \mathcal{H}_{\widetilde r_p}
\]
and
\[
\|F\circ\chi_p\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\widetilde r_p}}
\le
\|F\|_{\mathcal{H}_{r_p}}.
\]
<!-- end lemma-9 -->
<!-- proof-17: body_md -->
Since $a\in \mathcal{H}_{\rho_0}$, its restriction to the real axis is
automatically bounded above:
\[
a(x)\le \|a\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho_0}}.
\]
{}

Choose $0<r_p<\rho_0$ sufficiently small.  Cauchy's estimate and
$a(x)\ge a_0$ imply, after decreasing $r_p$ if necessary,
\[
|a(x+iy)-a(x)|\le \frac{a_0}{2},
\qquad |y|<r_p.
\]
Hence $a$ has no zeros in $\mathscr S_{r_p}$ and the branch $a^{-1/p}$ is
holomorphic there.  Since $a^{-1/p}$ is positive on $\mathbb R$, up to a
strip shrinkage, we have
\[
\Re q_p(z)\ge m_p>0.
\]

For $z_1,z_2\in \mathscr S_{r_p}$, convexity of the strip gives
\[
\frac{\eta_p(z_2)-\eta_p(z_1)}{z_2-z_1}
=
\int_0^1
q_p(z_1+\theta(z_2-z_1))\,d\theta ,
\]
so
\[
\Re
\frac{\eta_p(z_2)-\eta_p(z_1)}{z_2-z_1}
\ge m_p.
\]
Thus $\eta_p(z_1)\neq\eta_p(z_2)$ whenever $z_1\neq z_2$.

Moreover, since $\eta_p(x)\in\mathbb R$ for $x\in\mathbb R$,
\[
\Im\eta_p(x+iy)
=
\int_0^y\Re q_p(x+is)\,ds.
\]
Consequently,
\[
\Im\eta_p(x+ir_p)\ge m_pr_p,
\qquad
\Im\eta_p(x-ir_p)\le-m_pr_p.
\]
Also,
\[
\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\Re\eta_p(x+iy)
=
\Re q_p(x+iy)\ge m_p,
\]
so $\Re\eta_p(x+iy)\to\pm\infty$ as $x\to\pm\infty$,
uniformly for $|y|\le r_p$.
