Fix $w$ with $|\Im w|<m_pr_p$.  On the boundary of a sufficiently
large rectangle
\[
\Omega_X=\{z:\ |\Re z|<X,\ |\Im z|<r_p\},
\]
the curve $\eta_p(\partial\Omega_X)-w$ has winding number one about
the origin.  By the argument principle, $\eta_p(z)-w$ has (exacty) one zero
inside $\Omega_X$.                                                    
Thus
\[
S_{m_pr_p}\subset\eta_p(S_{r_p}),
\]
which proves the assertion.
<!-- end proof-17 -->
<!-- lemma-10: body_md -->
\label{lem:analytic-commutator}
\begin{equation}
 \|[W_\rho,g]u\|_2
 \le \rho M_1(g;\rho)\|W_\rho u\|_2.
\label{eq:analytic-commutator}
\end{equation}
<!-- end lemma-10 -->
<!-- proof-18: body_md -->
The operator $W_\rho$ is the Fourier multiplier with symbol
$e^{\rho|\xi|}$, whereas multiplication by $g$ corresponds on the
Fourier side to convolution with the finite complex measure
$\widehat g$.  Thus
\[
 \widehat{[W_\rho,g]u}(\xi)
 =
 \int_\mathbb R
 \bigl(e^{\rho|\xi|}-e^{\rho|\xi-\eta|}\bigr)
 \widehat u(\xi-\eta)\,d\widehat g(\eta).
\]
For real $a,b$ one has
$|e^a-e^b|\le |a-b|e^{\max\{a,b\}}$.  Hence
\[
 \bigl|e^{\rho|\xi|}-e^{\rho|\xi-\eta|}\bigr|
 \le
 \rho|\eta|e^{\rho|\eta|}e^{\rho|\xi-\eta|},
\tag{$\star$}\label{eq:analytic-exponential-difference}
\]
because
$\bigl||\xi|-|\xi-\eta|\bigr|\le|\eta|$ and
$\max\{|\xi|,|\xi-\eta|\}\le|\xi-\eta|+|\eta|$.
Taking absolute values and using \eqref{eq:analytic-exponential-difference} gives
\[
 |\widehat{[W_\rho,g]u}(\xi)|
 \le
 \rho\int_\mathbb R
 |\eta|e^{\rho|\eta|}
 |\widehat{W_\rho u}(\xi-\eta)|\,d|\widehat g|(\eta).
\]
Introduce the positive finite measure
\[
 d\mu(\eta):=|\eta|e^{\rho|\eta|}\,d|\widehat g|(\eta),
 \qquad
 \mu(\mathbb R)=M_1(g;\rho).
\]
Then
\[
 |\widehat{[W_\rho,g]u}|
 \le \rho\,\mu*|\widehat{W_\rho u}|.
\]
Young's inequality for convolution of an $L^2$ function with a finite
measure therefore yields
\[
 \|\widehat{[W_\rho,g]u}\|_2
 \le
 \rho\,\mu(\mathbb R)\|\widehat{W_\rho u}\|_2.
\]
By Plancherel,
\[
 \|[W_\rho,g]u\|_2
 \le \rho M_1(g;\rho)\|W_\rho u\|_2,
\]
which is \eqref{eq:analytic-commutator}.  Combining this with \eqref{eq:wiener-moment-estimate} gives
\[
 \|[W_\rho,g]u\|_2
 \le
 \frac{\rho}{R-\rho}\,
 \|g\|_{\mathfrak W_R}\|W_\rho u\|_2.
\]
<!-- end proof-18 -->
<!-- proposition-7: body_md -->
\label{prop:normalized-analytic-coefficients}
Assume the coefficient hypotheses of
Theorem~\ref{thm:unified-analytic-rate}.  Then, after restriction to
the strip $\mathscr S_{r_a}$ of Lemma~\ref{lem:analytic-liouville}, the
coefficients entering each normalized model are bounded and
holomorphic on a positive strip.

More precisely, in the Schr\"odinger case,
\[
F_2
:=
\frac{b}{\sqrt a},
\qquad 
G_2
:=
c-\frac12b'
+\frac12b\frac{a'}a
+i\left(
\frac14a''
-\frac{3}{16}\frac{(a')^2}{a}
\right)
\]
belong to $\mathcal H_{r_a}$, and
\[
v=F_2\circ\chi_2,
\qquad
w=G_2\circ\chi_2
\]
belong to $\mathcal H_{m_2r_a}$.

In the cubic case, with
\[
\alpha
=
-\frac23a''
+\frac59\frac{(a')^2}{a},
\qquad
\beta
=
-\frac13a'''
+\frac89\frac{a'a''}{a}
-\frac59\frac{(a')^3}{a^2},
\qquad
d=b-\alpha,
\qquad
V=(c-\beta)-\frac12d'
+\frac12d\frac{a'}a,
\]
the functions
\[
F_3:=d\,a^{-1/3},
\qquad
G_3:=V
\]
belong to $\mathcal H_{r_a}$, and
\[
v=F_3\circ\chi_3,
\qquad
w=G_3\circ\chi_3
\]
belong to $\mathcal H_{m_3r_a}$.

In both nonsmoothing cases $v$ is real-valued on $\mathbb R$.
<!-- end proposition-7 -->
<!-- proof-19: body_md -->
Since
\[
r_a\le \frac{\varrho_0}{4},
\]
all derivatives of $a,b,c$ of any fixed order are bounded on
$S_{r_a}$ by Cauchy's estimates on                 
$\mathscr S_{\varrho_0/2}$.
Furthermore,
\[
|a(z)|\ge\frac{a_0}{2},
\qquad z\in \mathscr S_{r_a},
\]
by Lemma~\ref{lem:analytic-liouville}.  Thus all reciprocal and
fractional-power expressions above are bounded and
holomorphic on $S_{r_a}$.

Lemma~\ref{lem:analytic-liouville} implies
\[
\chi_p(S_{m_pr_a})\subset S_{r_a}.
\]
Therefore composition with $\chi_p$ preserves bounded holomorphy on a smaller strip.  The reality of $v$ on $\mathbb R$ follows from
the reality of $a,b$ there.
<!-- end proof-19 -->
<!-- proposition-8: body_md -->
\label{prop:analytic-group}

Let
\[
L_{p,h}
=
P_{p,h}
+\frac12(vQ_h+Q_hv)+w,
\qquad p\in\{2,3\},
\]
where
\[
P_{2,h}=iQ_h^2,
\qquad
P_{3,h}=Q_h^3,
\]
and $Q_h$ is skew-adjoint with real symbol $iq_h(\xi)$ satisfying
\[
|q_h(\xi)|\le|\xi|.
\]

Assume that, for some $R>0$,
\[
v,w\in H^\infty(S_R),
\qquad
v(\bar z)=\overline{v(z)}.
\]
Fix $0<\rho_0<R$.  Then there exist
\[
C_0\ge1,\qquad \kappa,\Gamma\ge0,
\]
independent of $h$, such that
\begin{equation}
\|e^{tL_{p,h}}f\|_{\rho_0e^{-\kappa|t|}}
\le
C_0e^{\Gamma|t|}\|f\|_{\rho_0},
\qquad t\in\mathbb R.
\label{eq:analytic-group-propagation-new}
\end{equation}
The same estimate holds for $h=0$.
<!-- end proposition-8 -->
<!-- proposition-9: body_md -->
\label{prop:analytic-group}
Assume that $v$ is real-valued and $v,w\in\mathcal{H}_{\rho_*}$ for some
$\rho_*>0$.  Fix $0<\rho_0<\rho_*$.  Then there exist 
$C_0\ge 1$ and $\kappa,\Gamma\ge0$,
depending on $\rho_0$ and on the bounded analytic norms of $v,w$, but
independent of $h$, such
that
\begin{equation}
 \|e^{tL_{p,h}}f\|_{\rho_0e^{-\kappa|t|}}
 \le C_0 e^{\Gamma|t|}\|f\|_{\rho_0},
 \qquad t\in\mathbb R,
                                      
\label{eq:analytic-group-propagation-new}
\end{equation}
for $p=2,3$ and $h\in\mathbb R$.   
{}
<!-- end proposition-9 -->
<!-- proof-20: body_md -->
We divide the proof into three steps.

\medskip
\noindent
\emph{Step 1: a boundary-strip energy estimate for Galerkin
approximations.}
Let $h\ne0$.  
  
{}

                                                     
We use as analytic core the band-limited entire functions
\[
 \mathscr E:=\bigcup_{N\ge1}\operatorname{Ran}\Pi_N,
 \qquad
 \Pi_N:=\mathbf 1_{\{|\xi|\le N\}}(D).
\]
If $f\in\mathscr E$, then $\widehat f$ has compact support.  Hence the
inverse Fourier formula extends $f$ to an entire function on $\mathbb C$, and
for every $\rho>0$ and $s\ge0$ one has
$f\in\mathcal A_\rho^s$.  Moreover, $\mathscr E$ is dense in
$\mathcal A_{\rho_0}$, since
\[
 \|f-\Pi_Nf\|_{\rho_0}^2
 =\int_{|\xi|>N}e^{2\rho_0|\xi|}|\widehat f(\xi)|^2\,d\xi
 \longrightarrow0.
\]

We first establish the estimate on $\mathscr E$. {} Introduce an auxiliary
Fourier--Galerkin cutoff
\[
 L_{p,h}^{(M)}:=\Pi_ML_{p,h}\Pi_M,
 \qquad
 u_M(t):=e^{tL_{p,h}^{(M)}}f,
\]
with $M$ large enough that $f\in\operatorname{Ran}\Pi_M$.
The outer projection in $L_{p,h}^{(M)}$ makes
$\operatorname{Ran}\Pi_M$ invariant.  Indeed, if
$u\in\operatorname{Ran}\Pi_M$, then
\[
 L_{p,h}^{(M)}u=\Pi_ML_{p,h}u\in\operatorname{Ran}\Pi_M,
\]
and therefore the exponential series for the bounded operator
$L_{p,h}^{(M)}$ yields
\[
 u_M(t)=e^{tL_{p,h}^{(M)}}f\in\operatorname{Ran}\Pi_M
 \qquad (t\in\mathbb R).
\]
Equivalently,
$\operatorname{supp}\widehat{u_M(t)}\subset[-M,M]$ for every $t$. 

For $0<\rho<\rho_*$ define the two boundary traces
\[
U_\pm(t,x)
:=
u_M(t,x\pm i\rho(t))
\]
and the boundary energy
\begin{equation}
E(t)
:=
\|U_+(t)\|_2^2+\|U_-(t)\|_2^2.
\label{eq:boundary-energy}
\end{equation}
By Plancherel,
\begin{equation}
E(t)
=
2\int_{\mathbb R}
\cosh(2\rho(t)\xi)
|\widehat{u_M}(t,\xi)|^2\,d\xi.
\label{eq:boundary-energy-fourier}
\end{equation}
Likewise set
\begin{equation}
Z(t)
:=
\||D|^{1/2}U_+(t)\|_{L^2}^2
+
\||D|^{1/2}U_-(t)\|_{L^2}^2 .
\label{eq:boundary-half-energy}
\end{equation}

Fix for the moment one horizontal trace
\[
U_\sigma(x):=u_M(x+i\sigma),
\qquad |\sigma|<\rho_*.
\]

                                                          
   
                                    
   
                                                
   
            
   
             
   
          
  
           
  
        
  
          
   
    
   
          
  
          
  
       
  
          
   
The principal term $P_{p,h}$ is skew-adjoint on every horizontal line. 
Hence
\begin{equation}
\Re\langle P_{p,h}U_\sigma,U_\sigma\rangle=0.
\label{eq:principal-horizontal-skew}
\end{equation}
                                                                
                                                                      
                  

Consider now the first-order part
\[
B_h(v)
:=
\frac12(vQ_h+Q_hv).
\]
Write
\[
v(x+i\sigma)
=
\alpha_\sigma(x)+i\beta_\sigma(x),
\qquad
\alpha_\sigma,\beta_\sigma\in\mathbb R.
\]
Since $\alpha_\sigma$ is real,
\[
B_h(\alpha_\sigma)^*
=
-B_h(\alpha_\sigma),
\]
and therefore only $\beta_\sigma$ contributes to the real part:
\[
\Re\langle B_h(v_\sigma)U_\sigma,U_\sigma\rangle
=
\Re\langle iB_h(\beta_\sigma)U_\sigma,U_\sigma\rangle .
\]

Because $v$ is real on $\mathbb R$,
\[
\beta_\sigma(x)
=
\Im\bigl(v(x+i\sigma)-v(x)\bigr),
\]
and therefore
\begin{equation}
\|\beta_\sigma\|_\infty
\le
|\sigma|
\|v'\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho_1}},
\qquad
\|\beta_\sigma'\|_\infty
\le
|\sigma|
\|v''\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho_1}},
\label{eq:imaginary-v-strip}
\end{equation}
for every fixed
\[
\rho_0<\rho_1<\rho_*.
\]
                                                                 

By Lemma~\ref{lem:centered-transport-form},
\begin{align}
\left|
\Re\langle
B_h(v_\sigma)U_\sigma,U_\sigma
\rangle
\right|
&\le
C\|\beta_\sigma\|_\infty
\||D|^{1/2}U_\sigma\|_2^2
\nonumber\\
&\quad
+
C
\bigl(
\|\beta_\sigma\|_\infty
\|\beta_\sigma'\|_\infty
\bigr)^{1/2}
\||D|^{1/2}U_\sigma\|_2
\|U_\sigma\|_2 .
\label{eq:transport-boundary-form}
\end{align}
Using \eqref{eq:imaginary-v-strip} and Young's inequality, it follows
\begin{equation}
\left|
\Re\langle
B_h(v_\sigma)U_\sigma,U_\sigma
\rangle
\right|
\le
C_v|\sigma|
\left(
\||D|^{1/2}U_\sigma\|_2^2
+
\|U_\sigma\|_2^2
\right),
\label{eq:transport-boundary-final}
\end{equation}
where $C_v$ depends only on bounded strip norms of $v'$ and $v''$,
and is independent of $h$.

For the zeroth-order coefficient,
\begin{equation}
\Re\langle w_\sigma U_\sigma,U_\sigma\rangle
\le
\|w\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho_1}}\|U_\sigma\|_2^2.
\label{eq:w-boundary-estimate}
\end{equation}

We now look at the moving-boundary energy.
Since
\[
\frac{d}{dt}u_M(t,x+i\rho(t))
=
L_{p,h}u_M(t,x+i\rho(t))
+
i\dot\rho(t)Du_M(t,x+i\rho(t)),
\]
and
\[
\frac{d}{dt}u_M(t,x-i\rho(t))
=
L_{p,h}u_M(t,x-i\rho(t))
-
i\dot\rho(t)Du_M(t,x-i\rho(t)),
\]
the contributions caused by the motion of the two boundary lines sum
to
\begin{equation}
4\dot\rho(t)
\int_{\mathbb R}
|\xi|
\sinh(2\rho(t)|\xi|)
|\widehat{u_M}(t,\xi)|^2\,d\xi.
\label{eq:moving-boundary-term}
\end{equation}

Combining
\eqref{eq:principal-horizontal-skew},
\eqref{eq:transport-boundary-final},
\eqref{eq:w-boundary-estimate},
and \eqref{eq:moving-boundary-term}, we obtain
\begin{align}
\frac{d}{dt}E(t)
&\le
4\dot\rho(t)
\int_\mathbb R
|\xi|\sinh(2\rho(t)|\xi|)
|\widehat{u_M}(t,\xi)|^2\,d\xi
            
       
+C_v\rho(t)Z(t)
+C_1E(t),
\label{eq:boundary-energy-inequality}
\end{align}
where $C_1$ is independent of $M$ and $h$.

Choose
\begin{equation}
\rho(t)=\rho_0e^{-\kappa t},
\qquad t\ge0.
\label{eq:shrinking-strip}
\end{equation}
Then
\[
\dot\rho(t)=-\kappa\rho(t).
\]

Split frequency space into
\[
\Omega_{\rm lo}(t)
:=
\{\xi:2\rho(t)|\xi|<1\},
\qquad
\Omega_{\rm hi}(t)
:=
\{\xi:2\rho(t)|\xi|\ge1\}.
\]
On $\Omega_{\rm hi}(t)$,
\[
\sinh(2\rho|\xi|)
\ge
\tanh(1)\cosh(2\rho|\xi|).
\]
Hence, if $\kappa$ is chosen sufficiently large depending only on
$C_v$,
the negative contribution in
\eqref{eq:moving-boundary-term} absorbs the high-frequency part of
$C_v\rho Z$.

On the low-frequency region,
\[
\rho(t)|\xi|\le\frac12,
\]
and therefore
\begin{align*}
\rho(t)Z_{\rm lo}(t)
&=
2\rho(t)
\int_{\Omega_{\rm lo}(t)}
|\xi|\cosh(2\rho(t)\xi)
|\widehat{u_M}(t,\xi)|^2\,d\xi
\\
&\le
C E(t).
\end{align*}
Thus \eqref{eq:boundary-energy-inequality} reduces to
\begin{equation}
E'(t)\le C_2E(t),
\qquad t\ge0,
\label{eq:boundary-gronwall}
\end{equation}
where $C_2$ is independent of $M$ and $h$.
Gronwall's inequality yields
\begin{equation}
E(t)^{1/2}
\le
e^{C_2t/2}E(0)^{1/2}.
\label{eq:boundary-propagation}
\end{equation}

The boundary norm and the Fourier analytic norm are equivalent:
\[
\|g\|_\rho^2
\le
\|g(\cdot+i\rho)\|_{L^2}^2
+
\|g(\cdot-i\rho)\|_{L^2}^2
\le
2\|g\|_\rho^2.
\]
Consequently
\begin{equation}
\|e^{tL_{p,h}^{(M)}}f\|_{\rho_0e^{-\kappa t}}
\le
\sqrt2\,e^{\Gamma t}\|f\|_{\rho_0},
\qquad t\ge0,
\label{eq:galerkin-group-positive}
\end{equation}
for some $\Gamma\ge0$, uniformly in $M$ and $h$.

Applying the same argument to $-L_{p,h}^{(M)}$ gives
\begin{equation}
\|e^{tL_{p,h}^{(M)}}f\|_{\rho_0e^{-\kappa|t|}}
\le
\sqrt2\,e^{\Gamma|t|}\|f\|_{\rho_0},
\qquad t\in\mathbb R.
\label{eq:galerkin-group-two-sided}
\end{equation}
{}

\medskip
\noindent
\emph{Step 2: removal of the Galerkin cutoff for $h\ne0$.}

For fixed $h\ne0$, the multiplier $Q_h$ is bounded on $L^2$, hence
$L_{p,h}$ is a bounded operator on $L^2$ because $v,w\in L^\infty$.
Moreover
\[
L_{p,h}^{(M)}
=
\Pi_ML_{p,h}\Pi_M
\longrightarrow
L_{p,h}
\]
strongly on $L^2$.
The bounded-generator Duhamel formula therefore gives, for
$f\in\mathscr E$,
\[
e^{tL_{p,h}^{(M)}}f
\longrightarrow
e^{tL_{p,h}}f
\qquad
\text{in }L^2,
\]
uniformly for $t$ in compact intervals.

For fixed $t$, the norm
\[
g\longmapsto
\|g\|_{\rho_0e^{-\kappa|t|}}
\]
is lower semicontinuous with respect to $L^2$ convergence.
Passing to the limit in
\eqref{eq:galerkin-group-two-sided}, it follows
\begin{equation}
\|e^{tL_{p,h}}f\|_{\rho_0e^{-\kappa|t|}}
\le
\sqrt2\,e^{\Gamma|t|}\|f\|_{\rho_0},
\qquad
f\in\mathscr E.
\label{eq:analytic-group-propagation}
\end{equation}

{}

Next, we use density to extend it to $\mathcal A_{\rho_0}$.  Fix $h\ne0$ and $t\in\mathbb R$, write
\[
 U_h(t):=e^{tL_{p,h}},\qquad
 \rho_t:=\rho_0e^{-\kappa|t|},
\]
and let $f\in\mathcal A_{\rho_0}$.  Set $f_N:=\Pi_Nf\in\mathscr E$.
Then $f_N\to f$ in $\mathcal A_{\rho_0}$, and applying
\eqref{eq:analytic-group-propagation} to $f_N-f_M$ yields
\[
 \|U_h(t)(f_N-f_M)\|_{\rho_t}
 \le \sqrt{2} e^{\Gamma|t|}\|f_N-f_M\|_{\rho_0}.
\]
Hence $U_h(t)f_N$ converges in $\mathcal A_{\rho_t}$ to some $v(t)$.
Since $\mathcal A_{\rho_t}\hookrightarrow L^2$, the same sequence
converges to $v(t)$ in $L^2$.  But $U_h(t)$ is bounded on $L^2$ and
$f_N\to f$ in $L^2$, so $U_h(t)f_N\to U_h(t)f$ in $L^2$.
Therefore $v(t)=U_h(t)f$, and passing to the limit:
\[
 \|U_h(t)f\|_{\rho_t}
 \le e^{\Gamma|t|}\|f\|_{\rho_0}.
\]
Thus \eqref{eq:analytic-group-propagation-new} holds for every $f\in\mathcal A_{\rho_0}$.


\medskip
\noindent
\emph{Step 3: the differential generator case $h=0$.}

          
{
Set
\[
L:=L_{p,0},
\qquad
L_N:=\Pi_NL\Pi_N.
\]
Since $v$ is real on $\mathbb R$, the operator
\[
P_{p,0}+\frac12(vD+Dv)
\]
is skew-adjoint up to the standard closure, while $w$ is bounded.
Proposition~\ref{prop:sobolev-group-bound} therefore yields a
$C_0$-group
$
T(t):=e^{tL}
$
on $L^2$.                                

On $\operatorname{Ran}\Pi_N$, $L_N$ is bounded.  Moreover,
compression preserves the skew-adjoint part, and hence
\[
\|e^{tL_N}\|_{L^2\to L^2}
\le
e^{\|\Re w\|_\infty|t|}
\]
uniformly in $N$.
The boundary calculation of Step~1 applies verbatim to $L_N$, with
constants independent of $N$, and therefore
\begin{equation}
\|e^{tL_N}\Pi_Nf\|_{\rho_0e^{-\kappa|t|}}
\le
\sqrt2\,e^{\Gamma|t|}\|f\|_{\rho_0}.
\label{eq:galerkin-h0-analytic}
\end{equation}

Fix $f\in\mathcal A_{\rho_0}$ and $t\in\mathbb R$.  Since
\[
\mathcal A_{\rho_0}\subset H^p(\mathbb R),
\]
the orbit
\[
K_t:=\{T(s)f:\ |s|\le|t|\}
\]
is compact in $H^p(\mathbb R)$.
Define
\[
v_N(s):=e^{sL_N}\Pi_Nf,
\qquad
w_N(s):=\Pi_NT(s)f.
\]
Then
\[
v_N'(s)=L_Nv_N(s)
\]
and
\[
w_N'(s)
=
L_Nw_N(s)
+
\Pi_NL(I-\Pi_N)T(s)f.
\]
Hence
\[
v_N(t)-w_N(t)
=
-\int_0^t
e^{(t-s)L_N}
\Pi_NL(I-\Pi_N)T(s)f\,ds.
\]
Since
\[
L:H^p\longrightarrow L^2
\]
is bounded and $\Pi_N\to I$ strongly on $H^p$, uniformly on the
compact set $K_t$,
\[
\sup_{|s|\le|t|}
\|(I-\Pi_N)T(s)f\|_{H^p}
\longrightarrow0.
\]
Using the uniform $L^2$ bound for $e^{\tau L_N}$ therefore gives
\[
e^{tL_N}\Pi_Nf
\longrightarrow
e^{tL}f
\qquad
\text{in }L^2.
\]
Finally, lower semicontinuity of the analytic norm in
\eqref{eq:galerkin-h0-analytic} yields
\[
\|e^{tL}f\|_{\rho_0e^{-\kappa|t|}}
\le
\sqrt2\,e^{\Gamma|t|}\|f\|_{\rho_0}.
\]

Thus \eqref{eq:analytic-group-propagation-new} holds.                                   
}

{}
<!-- end proof-20 -->
<!-- proposition-10: body_md -->
\label{prop:analytic-parabolic}

Assume that, for some $\rho_*>0$,
\[
a,\varphi,p_0\in \mathcal{H}_{\rho_*},
\qquad
a(\bar z)=\overline{a(z)},
\qquad
\varphi(\bar z)=\overline{\varphi(z)},
\]
and
\[
a(x)\ge a_0>0,
\qquad x\in\mathbb R.
\]
Then, there exists $0<\rho_1<\rho_*$ such that
\[
\Re a(z)\ge \frac{a_0}{2},
\qquad z\in \mathscr S_{\rho_1},
\]
 
and, for every $0<\rho_0<\rho_1$ there exist $C_0,\Gamma\ge0$,
independent of $h$, such that
\begin{equation}
\|e^{tA_h}f\|_{\rho_0}
\le
C_0e^{\Gamma t}\|f\|_{\rho_0},
\qquad t\ge0.
\label{eq:analytic-parabolic-propagation-new}
\end{equation}
The same estimate holds for $A=A_0$.
<!-- end proposition-10 -->
<!-- proposition-11: body_md -->
\label{prop:analytic-parabolic}
Let
\[
A
=
DaD+\frac12(\varphi D+D\varphi)+p_0,
\qquad
\varphi=b-a',
\qquad
p_0=c+\frac12(a''-b'),
\]
and
\[
A_h
=
\delta_h a\delta_h
+
\frac12(\varphi\delta_h+\delta_h\varphi)
+p_0.
\]
Assume that, for some $R>0$,
\[
a,\varphi,p_0\in H^\infty(S_R),
\qquad
a(\bar z)=\overline{a(z)},
\]
and
\[
a(x)\ge a_0>0,
\qquad x\in\mathbb R.
\]
Then there exists $0<R_1<R$ such that
\[
\Re a(z)\ge \frac{a_0}{2},
\qquad z\in S_{R_1}.
\]
For every $0<\rho_0<R_1$ there exist constants
$C_0\ge1$ and $\Gamma\ge0$, independent of $h$, such that
\begin{equation}
\|e^{tA_h}f\|_{\rho_0}
\le
C_0e^{\Gamma t}\|f\|_{\rho_0},
\qquad
t\ge0.
\label{eq:analytic-parabolic-propagation}
\end{equation}
The same estimate holds for $A=A_0$.
<!-- end proposition-11 -->
<!-- proof-21: body_md -->
Since $a$ is bounded and holomorphic on $\mathscr S_{\rho_*}$, Cauchy's estimate on
a slightly smaller strip gives
\[
\sup_{x\in\mathbb R}
|a(x+iy)-a(x)|
\le
C|y|,
\]
uniformly for sufficiently small $|y|$.
Since
\[
a(x)\ge a_0>0
\qquad (x\in\mathbb R),
\]
there exist $0<\rho_1<\rho_*$ such that
\begin{equation}
\Re a(z)\ge a_*:=\frac{a_0}{2},
\qquad
z\in \mathscr S_{\rho_1}.
\label{eq:complex-strip-ellipticity}
\end{equation}

As in Proposition~\ref{prop:analytic-group}, let
\[
\mathscr E
=
\bigcup_{N\ge1}\operatorname{Ran}\Pi_N.
\]
Fix first $h\ne0$, $f\in\mathscr E$, and introduce
\[
A_h^{(M)}
=
\Pi_MA_h\Pi_M,
\qquad
u_M(t)
=
e^{tA_h^{(M)}}f.
\]
The function $u_M(t)$ remains band limited, so its boundary traces are
entire. {}

Let $0<\rho_0<\rho_1$ and fix $\sigma$ with $|\sigma|\le\rho_0$ and put
\[
U_\sigma(t,x)
:=
u_M(t,x+i\sigma).
\]
Since $\delta_h$ is a constant-coefficient operator,
\[
(\delta_hu_M)(x+i\sigma)
=
\delta_hU_\sigma(x).
\]
Furthermore
\[
\delta_h^*=-\delta_h
\]
on $L^2(\mathbb R+i\sigma)$.

Set
\[
a_\sigma(x):=a(x+i\sigma),
\qquad
\varphi_\sigma(x):=\varphi(x+i\sigma),
\qquad
p_{0,\sigma}(x):=p_0(x+i\sigma).
\]
The principal quadratic form satisfies
\begin{align}
\Re\langle
\delta_h(a_\sigma\delta_hU_\sigma),
U_\sigma
\rangle
&=
-\Re\langle
a_\sigma\delta_hU_\sigma,
\delta_hU_\sigma
\rangle
\nonumber\\
&=
-\int_\mathbb R
\Re a_\sigma(x)
|\delta_hU_\sigma(x)|^2\,dx
\nonumber\\
&\le
-a_*
\|\delta_hU_\sigma\|_2^2,
\label{eq:parabolic-strip-principal}
\end{align}
by \eqref{eq:complex-strip-ellipticity}.

For the symmetrized first-order term,
\begin{align*}
&
\left|
\left\langle
\frac12
(\varphi_\sigma\delta_h+\delta_h\varphi_\sigma)
U_\sigma,
U_\sigma
\right\rangle
\right|
\\
&\qquad
\le
\frac12
\left|
\langle
\varphi_\sigma\delta_hU_\sigma,
U_\sigma
\rangle
\right|
+
\frac12
\left|
\langle
\delta_h(\varphi_\sigma U_\sigma),
U_\sigma
\rangle
\right|.
\end{align*}
Using $\delta_h^*=-\delta_h$ in the second term yields
\[
\left|
\left\langle
\frac12
(\varphi_\sigma\delta_h+\delta_h\varphi_\sigma)
U_\sigma,
U_\sigma
\right\rangle
\right|
\le
\|\varphi\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho_1}}
\|\delta_hU_\sigma\|_2
\|U_\sigma\|_2,
\]
                                   
                             
whence
\begin{equation}
\left|
\Re
\left\langle
\frac12
(\varphi_\sigma\delta_h+\delta_h\varphi_\sigma)
U_\sigma,
U_\sigma
\right\rangle
\right|
\le
\frac{a_*}{2}
\|\delta_hU_\sigma\|_2^2
+
C_\varphi\|U_\sigma\|_2^2,
\label{eq:parabolic-strip-first-order}
\end{equation}
where
$
C_\varphi
=
{\|\varphi\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho_1}}^2}/{(2a_*)}.
$ 
                        

Finally,
\begin{equation}
\Re\langle
p_{0,\sigma}U_\sigma,U_\sigma
\rangle
\le
\|p_0\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho_1}}
\|U_\sigma\|_2^2.
\label{eq:parabolic-strip-zero}
\end{equation}

Combining
\eqref{eq:parabolic-strip-principal},
\eqref{eq:parabolic-strip-first-order},
and \eqref{eq:parabolic-strip-zero}, we obtain
\begin{equation}
\frac12\frac{d}{dt}
\|U_\sigma(t)\|_2^2
+
\frac{a_*}{2}
\|\delta_hU_\sigma(t)\|_2^2
\le
\Gamma
\|U_\sigma(t)\|_2^2,
\label{eq:parabolic-boundary-energy}
\end{equation}
where
\[
\Gamma
:=
C_\varphi+\|p_0\|_{\mathcal{H}_{\rho_1}},
\]
 is independent of $h$, $M$, and $\sigma$.

Dropping the nonnegative dissipative term and applying Gronwall yields
\begin{equation}
\|U_\sigma(t)\|_{L^2}
\le
e^{\Gamma t}
\|U_\sigma(0)\|_{L^2},
\qquad
t\ge0.
\label{eq:parabolic-boundary-gronwall}
\end{equation}
Applying this with
$
\sigma=\rho_0
\ \text{and}\ 
\sigma=-\rho_0,
$
                       
we obtain,                                
\[
\|e^{tA_h^{(M)}}f\|_{\partial,\rho_0}
\le
e^{\Gamma t}
\|f\|_{\partial,\rho_0}.
\]
By the norm equivalence
\[
\|g\|_{\rho_0}
\le
\|g\|_{\partial,\rho_0}
\le
\sqrt2\,\|g\|_{\rho_0},
\]
we obtain
\begin{equation}
\|e^{tA_h^{(M)}}f\|_{\rho_0}
\le
\sqrt2\,e^{\Gamma t}\|f\|_{\rho_0},
\qquad
t\ge0,
\label{eq:parabolic-galerkin-analytic}
\end{equation}
uniformly in $M$ and $h$.

For fixed $h\ne0$, $A_h$ is bounded on $L^2$ and
\[
A_h^{(M)}
=
\Pi_MA_h\Pi_M
\longrightarrow A_h
\]
strongly on $L^2$ as $M\to \infty$.
The bounded-generator Duhamel formula gives
\[
e^{tA_h^{(M)}}f
\longrightarrow
e^{tA_h}f
\qquad\text{in }L^2
\]
for every $f\in\mathscr E$ and fixed $t\ge0$.
Lower semicontinuity of the analytic norm in
\eqref{eq:parabolic-galerkin-analytic} therefore yields
\[
\|e^{tA_h}f\|_{\rho_0}
\le
\sqrt2\,e^{\Gamma t}\|f\|_{\rho_0},
\qquad
f\in\mathscr E.
\]
Density of $\mathscr E$ in $\mathcal A_{\rho_0}$ extends this estimate
to every $f\in\mathcal A_{\rho_0}$.

It remains to treat the case $h=0$.  Set
\[
A^{(N)}
:=
\Pi_NA\Pi_N.
\]
On the real line the principal quadratic form is dissipative:
\[
\Re\langle DaDu,u\rangle
=
-\int_\mathbb R a(x)|Du(x)|^2\,dx
\le0.
\]
Since $\varphi=b-a'$ is real on $\mathbb R$, then
\[
\frac12(\varphi D+D\varphi)
\]
is skew-symmetric. {}

Thus
\[
\|e^{tA^{(N)}}\|_{L^2\to L^2}
\le
e^{\omega t}
\]
with $\omega$ independent of $N$.
The boundary calculation above applies to $A^{(N)}$ with the same
constants and gives
\[
\|e^{tA^{(N)}}\Pi_Nf\|_{\rho_0}
\le
\sqrt2\,e^{\Gamma t}\|f\|_{\rho_0}.
\]

The uniformly elliptic operator $A$ generates the required analytic
semigroup on $L^2$, with domain $H^2(\mathbb R)$. {}  For
$f\in\mathcal A_{\rho_0}\subset H^2(\mathbb R)$, the orbit
\[
\{e^{sA}f:0\le s\le t\}
\]
is compact in $H^2(\mathbb R)$ for each fixed $t>0$.  Since
\[
A:H^2(\mathbb R)\longrightarrow L^2(\mathbb R)
\]
is bounded and $\Pi_N\to I$ strongly on $H^2$, the same
variation-of-constants argument as in
Proposition~\ref{prop:analytic-group} gives
\[
e^{tA^{(N)}}\Pi_Nf
\longrightarrow
e^{tA}f
\qquad
\text{in }L^2.
\]
Lower semicontinuity therefore yields
\[
\|e^{tA}f\|_{\rho_0}
\le
\sqrt2\,e^{\Gamma t}\|f\|_{\rho_0}.
\]
This concludes the proof of \eqref{eq:analytic-parabolic-propagation-new}, with
$C_0=\sqrt2$.
<!-- end proof-21 -->
<!-- proposition-12: body_md -->
\label{prop:analytic-parabolic}
Assume $a$ is real, $a(x)\ge a_0>0$, and
$a,\varphi,p_0\in\mathfrak W_R$ for some $R>0$.  Choose
$0<\rho_0<R$ sufficiently small that
\begin{equation}
 \rho_0M_1(a;\rho_0)\le \frac{a_0}{2}.
\label{eq:parabolic-dissipation}
\end{equation}
Then there exist $\kappa,\Gamma\ge0$, independent of $h$, such that
\begin{equation}
 \|e^{tA_h}f\|_{\rho_0e^{-\kappa t}}
 \le e^{\Gamma t}\|f\|_{\rho_0},
 \qquad t\ge0.
\label{eq:analytic-parabolic-propagation}
\end{equation}
The same estimate holds for $A=A_0$.
<!-- end proposition-12 -->
<!-- proof-22: body_md -->
We use the same dense analytic core
$\mathscr E=\bigcup_{N\ge1}\operatorname{Ran}\Pi_N$ as in
Proposition~\ref{prop:analytic-group}.  Fix first $h\ne0$ and
$f\in\mathscr E$.  As above, 
{}
 we
introduce
\[
 A_h^{(M)}:=\Pi_MA_h\Pi_M,
 \qquad
 u_M(t):=e^{tA_h^{(M)}}f,
\]
with $M$ large enough that $f\in\operatorname{Ran}\Pi_M$.  The
space $\operatorname{Ran}\Pi_M$ is invariant under $A_h^{(M)}$, so
$u_M(t)$ remains band limited.  By the Fourier-support estimate proved
in Step~1 of Proposition~\ref{prop:analytic-group},
\[
 \|u_M(t)\|_{\rho,s}
 \le e^{\rho M}\langle M\rangle^s\|u_M(t)\|_2,
 \qquad \rho,s\ge0.
\]
Thus $u_M(t)$ and $U_M=W_{\rho(t)}u_M(t)$ have all the analytic--Sobolev
regularity required for differentiating the energy.  Since
$\Pi_M$ commutes with $\delta_h$ and $W_\rho$, and since
$U_M\in\operatorname{Ran}\Pi_M$, the projections disappear from the
quadratic forms exactly as in Step~1.  We may therefore write $u,U$ in
place of $u_M,U_M$ and set $V=\delta_hU$.  Since $W_\rho$ commutes
with $\delta_h$ and $\delta_h^*=-\delta_h$,
\begin{align*}
 \operatorname{Re}\langle W_\rho\delta_h a\delta_hu,U\rangle
 &=-\langle aV,V\rangle
   -\operatorname{Re}\langle[W_\rho,a]\delta_hu,V\rangle\\
 &\le
 -\bigl(a_0-\rho M_1(a;\rho)\bigr)\|V\|_2^2
 \le -\frac{a_0}{2}\|V\|_2^2,
\end{align*}
where \eqref{eq:analytic-commutator} and \eqref{eq:parabolic-dissipation} were used.  The skew first-order term satisfies
the analogue of \eqref{eq:analytic-energy-identity},
\[
 \left|\operatorname{Re}\left\langle
 W_\rho\frac12(\varphi\delta_h+\delta_h\varphi)u,U
 \right\rangle\right|
 \le C\rho M_1(\varphi;\rho)Z
    +C\rho M_2(\varphi;\rho)\|U\|_2^2,
\]
with $Z=\||D|^{1/2}U\|_2^2$, and multiplication by $p_0$ contributes
at most $M_0(p_0;\rho)\|U\|_2^2$.  Consequently
\begin{align*}
 \frac12\frac d{dt}\|U\|_2^2
 &+\frac{a_0}{2}\|\delta_hU\|_2^2\\
 &\le
 \bigl(\dot\rho+C\rho M_1(\varphi;\rho)\bigr)Z
 +\bigl(C\rho M_2(\varphi;\rho)+M_0(p_0;\rho)\bigr)\|U\|_2^2.
\end{align*}
Choose $\rho(t)=\rho_0e^{-\kappa t}$ with
$\kappa\ge2CM_1(\varphi;\rho_0)$.  Then
$\dot\rho(t)=-\kappa\rho(t)$ and, since $\rho(t)\le\rho_0$ and
$M_1(\varphi;\rho)$ is increasing in $\rho$,
\[
 \dot\rho+C\rho M_1(\varphi;\rho)
 =\rho\bigl(-\kappa+CM_1(\varphi;\rho)\bigr)
 \le -\frac{\kappa}{2}\rho\le0.
\]
Thus the $Z$ term can be discarded.  We may also drop the {}
term $\frac{a_0}{2}\|\delta_hU\|_2^2$ from the left-hand side.  Since
$\rho(t)\le\rho_0$ and $M_j(g;\rho)$ is increasing in $\rho$,
\[
 C\rho(t)M_2(\varphi;\rho(t))+M_0(p_0;\rho(t))
 \le
 C\rho_0M_2(\varphi;\rho_0)+M_0(p_0;\rho_0)=:\Gamma.
\]
Hence
\[
 \frac12\frac d{dt}\|U(t)\|_2^2
 \le \Gamma\|U(t)\|_2^2.
\]
Gronwall's inequality gives
\[
 \|U(t)\|_2\le e^{\Gamma t}\|U(0)\|_2.
\]
Recalling that $U(t)=W_{\rho(t)}e^{tA_h^{(M)}}f$ and
$U(0)=W_{\rho_0}f$, we obtain;
\begin{equation}
 \|e^{tA_h^{(M)}}f\|_{\rho_0e^{-\kappa t}}
 \le e^{\Gamma t}\|f\|_{\rho_0},
 \qquad t\ge0.
\label{eq:analytic-parabolic-aux}
\end{equation}
The constants $\kappa$ and $\Gamma$ depend only on $\rho_0$ and the
coefficient moments appearing above; in particular, they are
independent of the Galerkin cutoff $M$ (and of $h$).
For fixed $h\ne0$, $A_h$ is bounded on $L^2$ and
$A_h^{(M)}=\Pi_MA_h\Pi_M\to A_h$ strongly on $L^2$; hence the
bounded-generator Duhamel formula gives
\[
 e^{tA_h^{(M)}}f\longrightarrow e^{tA_h}f
 \qquad\text{in }L^2
\]
for each fixed $t\ge0$ and $f\in\mathscr E$.  Lower
semicontinuity of the weighted norm therefore proves \eqref{eq:analytic-parabolic-propagation}
on $\mathscr E$.  Finally, if $f\in\mathcal A_{\rho_0}$, then
$f_N:=\Pi_Nf\in\mathscr E$ and
$f_N\to f$ in $\mathcal A_{\rho_0}$.  Applying \eqref{eq:analytic-parabolic-propagation}
to $f_N-f_M$ and identifying the resulting analytic-norm limit with
$e^{tA_h}f$ through $L^2$ convergence extends the estimate to all
$f\in\mathcal A_{\rho_0}$.

For the operator $A=A_0$, use the same fixed-time
Fourier--Galerkin passage as in Step~2 of
Proposition~\ref{prop:analytic-group}, with
$A^{(N)}=\Pi_NA\Pi_N$.  Compression preserves the negative elliptic
quadratic form and the skew first-order part, so the analytic estimate
is uniform in $N$.  Moreover $A:H^2\to L^2$ is bounded and, for
$f\in\mathcal A_{\rho_0}\subset H^2$, the semigroup orbit is
continuous in $H^2$.  For each fixed $t\ge0$, compactness of the orbit
segment $\{e^{sA}f:0\le s\le t\}$ in $H^2$ gives
\[
 \sup_{0\le s\le t}
 \|(I-\Pi_N)e^{sA}f\|_{H^2}\longrightarrow0.
\]
The corresponding variation-of-constants formula therefore yields
\[
 e^{tA^{(N)}}\Pi_Nf\longrightarrow e^{tA}f
 \qquad\text{in }L^2
\]
at that fixed time.  Lower semicontinuity of the weighted norm then
gives \eqref{eq:analytic-parabolic-propagation} for $A=A_0$. 
{}
<!-- end proof-22 -->
<!-- lemma-11: body_md -->
\label{lem:analytic-defects}
For each of the three centered-difference models, let $p=2$ for the
parabolic and Schr\"odinger cases and $p=3$ for the cubic case.  Under
the analytic coefficient hypotheses above, there exist $A_*,B_*>0$
such that, for $0<\rho'<\rho\le\rho_0$,
\begin{equation}
 \|R_rf\|_{\rho'}
 \le
 A_*B_*^r
 \frac{\Gamma(2r+p+1)}{(\rho-\rho')^{2r+p}}
 \|f\|_\rho,
 \qquad r\ge1.
\label{eq:analytic-defect-bound}
\end{equation}
Moreover, if
\[
 A_h=A+\sum_{r=1}^{m-1}h^{2r}R_r+h^{2m}Q_m(h),
\]
then there exist model-dependent constants $\theta_*,A_1,B_1>0$,
independent of $m$, $h$, $\rho$, and $\rho'$, such that
\[
 \|Q_m(h)f\|_{\rho'}
 \le A_1B_1^m(\rho-\rho')^{-(2m+p)}\|f\|_\rho
\]
whenever $|h|\le\theta_*(\rho-\rho')$. 
{}
<!-- end lemma-11 -->
<!-- proof-23: body_md -->
Set
\[
 \Delta:=\rho-\rho'>0.
\]
We separate the proof into three steps.

\medskip
{}

\medskip
\noindent\emph{Step 1: structure and combinatorics of the coefficient $R_r$.}
Recall that
\begin{equation}
 \delta_h
 =\sum_{q=0}^{\infty}\frac{h^{2q}}{(2q+1)!}D^{2q+1}.
\label{eq:analytic-centered-series}
\end{equation}
Consider one fixed monomial occurring in the centered generator $A_h$,
and let $k$ be the number of occurrences of $\delta_h$ in that
monomial.  By the structure of the three models,
\begin{equation}
 k\le p.
\label{eq:analytic-word-structure}
\end{equation}
{}

To obtain a contribution of order $h^{2r}$ from a monomial containing
$k$ centered differences, choose integers
\[
 q_1,\dots,q_k\ge0,
 \qquad q_1+\cdots+q_k=r.
\]
The corresponding derivative factors are
$D^{2q_1+1},\dots,D^{2q_k+1}$, and hence their total differential order
is
\begin{equation}
 d=\sum_{j=1}^k(2q_j+1)=2r+k\le2r+p.
\label{eq:analytic-word-order}
\end{equation}
                                                                  
Every such contribution can therefore be written as a noncommutative word
\begin{equation}
 \mathcal W
 =M_{g_s}D^{d_s}M_{g_{s-1}}D^{d_{s-1}}\cdots
  M_{g_1}D^{d_1}M_{g_0},
 \qquad d_1+\cdots+d_s=d\le2r+p,
\label{eq:analytic-word-form}
\end{equation}
where the $g_j$ belong to the finite collection of analytic
coefficients appearing in the model. 
{}

For fixed $k$, the number of choices of
$q_1+\cdots+q_k=r$ is the weak-composition number
$\binom{r+k-1}{k-1}$.  Since $k\le p$ and the generator contains only
finitely many structural monomials, there exists a model-dependent
constant $C_0>1$ such that the total number of contributions to $R_r$
is bounded by $C_0^r$.
{}  
The Taylor denominators
$\prod_j(2q_j+1)!^{-1}$ in \eqref{eq:analytic-centered-series} are at most one and hence
can only improve the estimates below.

\medskip
\noindent\emph{Step 2: estimate of a single word and proof of
\eqref{eq:analytic-defect-bound}.}
Fix a word \eqref{eq:analytic-word-form} and put
$d=d_1+\cdots+d_s$.  Allocate the available strip width proportionally
to the positive derivative orders:
\begin{equation}
 \Delta_j:=\Delta\frac{d_j}{d},
 \qquad \sum_{j=1}^s\Delta_j=\Delta.
\label{eq:analytic-radius-allocation}
\end{equation}
Starting from the right of \eqref{eq:analytic-word-form}, apply successively the
multiplication estimate \eqref{eq:analytic-multiplier} and the derivative estimate
\eqref{eq:analytic-derivative-block}, spending the radius loss $\Delta_j$ on the block
$D^{d_j}$.  Since the number of coefficient multipliers in each
structural monomial is bounded independently of $r$, there exists a
constant $C_1$, depending only on the model and the analytic coefficient
norms, such that
\begin{align*}
 \|\mathcal Wf\|_{\rho'}
 &\le C_1
 \prod_{j=1}^s\frac{d_j!}{\Delta_j^{d_j}}\|f\|_\rho\\
 &=C_1\Delta^{-d}d^d
   \prod_{j=1}^s\frac{d_j!}{d_j^{d_j}}\|f\|_\rho\\
 &\le C_1\Delta^{-d}d^d\|f\|_\rho,
\end{align*}
since $d_j!\le d_j^{d_j}$.  The elementary bound
$d^d\le e^d d!=e^d\Gamma(d+1)$ therefore yields
\begin{equation}
 \|\mathcal Wf\|_{\rho'}
 \le C_1e^d\frac{\Gamma(d+1)}{\Delta^d}\|f\|_\rho.
\label{eq:analytic-word-factor-bound}
\end{equation}
Because $d\le2r+p$,
$\Gamma(d+1)\le\Gamma(2r+p+1)$.  Moreover,
\[
 \Delta^{-d}
 =\Delta^{2r+p-d}\Delta^{-(2r+p)},
\]
and, since $0<\Delta\le\rho_0$ and $2r+p-d\ge0$,
\[
 \Delta^{2r+p-d}
 \le \max\{1,\rho_0\}^{2r+p}.
\]
Together with $e^d\le e^{2r+p}$, this gives constants $C_2,C_3>0$,
independent of $r$, $\rho$, and $\rho'$, such that
\begin{equation}
 \|\mathcal Wf\|_{\rho'}
 \le C_2C_3^r
 \frac{\Gamma(2r+p+1)}{\Delta^{2r+p}}\|f\|_\rho.
\label{eq:analytic-word-bound}
\end{equation}
Summing over the at most $C_0^r$ structural/composition terms and
absorbing the resulting geometric factor into $B_*^r$ proves
\eqref{eq:analytic-defect-bound}.

\medskip
\noindent\emph{Step 3: the terminal remainder $Q_m(h)$.}
The terminal remainder should not be estimated by summing
\eqref{eq:analytic-defect-bound}, because that would bring the factorial factor
$\Gamma(2r+p+1)$ into the tail.  Instead we exploit holomorphic
dependence on
\[
 z:=h^2.
\]
Define
\begin{equation}
 \mathfrak D(z)
 :=\sum_{q=0}^{\infty}\frac{z^q}{(2q+1)!}D^{2q+1}.
\label{eq:analytic-remainder-derivative}
\end{equation}
Let $0<\sigma'<\sigma$ and set $\delta:=\sigma-\sigma'$.  By
\eqref{eq:analytic-cauchy-fourier},
\[
 \left\|
 \frac{z^q}{(2q+1)!}D^{2q+1}f
 \right\|_{\sigma'}
 \le
 \frac1\delta
 \left(\frac{|z|}{\delta^2}\right)^q\|f\|_\sigma.
\]
Hence, for $|z|<\delta^2$,
\begin{equation}
 \|\mathfrak D(z)\|_{\mathcal A_\sigma\to\mathcal A_{\sigma'}}
 \le
 \frac1\delta\frac1{1-|z|/\delta^2}.
\label{eq:analytic-remainder-block}
\end{equation}
The series converges normally on smaller disks, so
$z\mapsto\mathfrak D(z)$ is holomorphic as an
$\mathcal L(\mathcal A_\sigma,\mathcal A_{\sigma'})$-valued map.  For
real $z=h^2$ one has $\mathfrak D(h^2)=\delta_h$.

Let $\mathfrak A(z)$ be the full centered generator obtained from
$A_h$ by replacing every occurrence of $\delta_h$ by
$\mathfrak D(z)$.  Every structural monomial contains at most $p$ such
factors by \eqref{eq:analytic-word-structure}.  Split the total strip loss
$\Delta=\rho-\rho'$ into $p$ equal pieces.  Each occurrence of
$\mathfrak D(z)$ is then assigned a radius loss at least $\Delta/p$,
whereas multiplication by the analytic coefficients costs no radius.
It follows from \eqref{eq:analytic-remainder-block} that
\[
 \mathfrak A(z):\mathcal A_\rho\longrightarrow\mathcal A_{\rho'}
\]
is holomorphic for $|z|<(\Delta/p)^2$.

Choose
\[
 R_\Delta:=\frac{\Delta^2}{4p^2}.
\]
On $|z|=R_\Delta$ one has
$|z|/(\Delta/p)^2=1/4$, and therefore each factor
$\mathfrak D(z)$ is bounded by $C_p\Delta^{-1}$.  Since there are at
most $p$ such factors in any monomial, and since all coefficient
multipliers are uniformly bounded on the intermediate strips,
\begin{equation}
 \sup_{|z|=R_\Delta}
 \|\mathfrak A(z)\|_{\mathcal A_\rho\to\mathcal A_{\rho'}}
 \le C\Delta^{-p}.
\label{eq:analytic-remainder-word-bound}
\end{equation}
Terms containing fewer than $p$ centered-difference factors satisfy an
initial bound $C\Delta^{-k}$ with $k<p$; because
$\Delta\le\rho_0$, this is also absorbed into the right-hand side of
\eqref{eq:analytic-remainder-word-bound} after changing $C$.

The Taylor coefficients of $\mathfrak A$ at $z=0$ are precisely
$A,R_1,R_2,\ldots$.  Thus, for fixed $m$,
\begin{equation}
 \mathfrak A(z)
 =A+\sum_{r=1}^{m-1}z^rR_r+z^mQ_m(z).
\label{eq:analytic-generator-remainder}
\end{equation}
Cauchy's integral formula for the Taylor remainder, applied in the
Banach space $\mathcal L(\mathcal A_\rho,\mathcal A_{\rho'})$, gives
\begin{equation}
 Q_m(z)
 =\frac1{2\pi i}\int_{|\zeta|=R_\Delta}
 \frac{\mathfrak A(\zeta)}{\zeta^m(\zeta-z)}\,d\zeta.
\label{eq:analytic-terminal-bound}
\end{equation}
If $|z|\le R_\Delta/2$, then
$|\zeta-z|\ge R_\Delta/2$ on the contour.  Using
\eqref{eq:analytic-remainder-word-bound},
\begin{align*}
 \|Q_m(z)f\|_{\rho'}
 &\le 2C\Delta^{-p}R_\Delta^{-m}\|f\|_\rho\\
 &=2C(4p^2)^m\Delta^{-(2m+p)}\|f\|_\rho.
\end{align*}
Hence there are model-dependent constants $A_1,B_1>0$, independent of
$m$, $z$, $\rho$, and $\rho'$, such that
\[
 \|Q_m(z)f\|_{\rho'}
 \le A_1B_1^m\Delta^{-(2m+p)}\|f\|_\rho.
\]
Finally set $z=h^2$.  The condition $|z|\le R_\Delta/2$ follows by imposing
\[
 |h|\le\theta_*\Delta,
 \qquad
 \theta_*:=\frac1{2\sqrt2\,p}.
\]
Since the real centered-difference remainder $Q_m(h)$ is precisely the
operator $Q_m(h^2)$ in \eqref{eq:analytic-generator-remainder}, the asserted terminal estimate
follows.

{}
<!-- end proof-23 -->
<!-- theorem-11: body_md -->
\label{thm:analytic-bridge}
Assume the hypotheses of Proposition~\ref{prop:analytic-parabolic} in
the parabolic case, or Proposition~\ref{prop:analytic-group} in the
normalized Schr\"odinger/cubic cases, and let
$f\in\mathcal A_{\rho_0}$.  Let $p=2$ in the two second-order cases and
$p=3$ in the cubic case.  Then for every $T>0$ and every
$0<\rho_*<\rho_1e^{-\kappa T}$ there exist constants $C_T,K_T>0$ and
$N_T\ge1$, independent of $f$, $m$, and $N$, such that, for every $N\ge N_T$ and every $0\le t\le T$,
\begin{equation}
 \left\|
 \mathcal S_2(t/N)^Nf-e^{tA}f
 -\sum_{r=1}^{m-1}\frac{E_{2r}(t)f}{N^{2r}}
 \right\|_{\rho_*}
 \le
 C_TK_T^m\Gamma((p+1)m+1)e^{\Gamma t}
 \frac{t^{2m+1}}{N^{2m}}\,
 \|f\|_{\rho_0}.
\label{eq:analytic-bridge-estimate}
\end{equation}
In particular, the analytic product expansion realizes the pointwise
form \eqref{eq:product-expansion} on every fixed strip interval, with
\[
        
 {\ C^{\rm an}_{m,T}\le C_TK_T^m\Gamma((p+1)m+1).                         
 \ }
\]
{}
<!-- end theorem-11 -->
<!-- proof-24: body_md -->
Write
\[
 T(t):=e^{tA},\qquad T_h(t):=e^{tA_h},\qquad
 \Delta_h:=A_h-A.
\]
For every integer $q\ge1$, Lemma~\ref{lem:analytic-defects} gives the
finite generator expansion
\begin{equation}
 \Delta_h
 =\sum_{j=1}^{q-1}h^{2j}R_j+h^{2q}Q_q(h).
\label{eq:analytic-generator-depth-expansion}
\end{equation}
We use this expansion recursively, choosing at each stage only the
remaining depth needed to reach total weight $m$.

\medskip
\noindent\emph{Step 1: a fixed strip reserve on $[0,T]$.}
The proofs of Propositions~\ref{prop:analytic-group} and
\ref{prop:analytic-parabolic}                                                    
yields the following propagation estimate.  
With the same constants
$\kappa,\Gamma$, for every $0<\sigma\le\rho_1$ and every
$0\le\tau\le T$,
\begin{equation}
 \|T(\tau)g\|_{\sigma e^{-\kappa\tau}}
 \le C_0 e^{\Gamma\tau}\|g\|_\sigma,
 \qquad
 \|T_h(\tau)g\|_{\sigma e^{-\kappa\tau}}
 \le C_0 e^{\Gamma\tau}\|g\|_\sigma,
\label{eq:analytic-propagator-bound}
\end{equation}
uniformly in the admissible $h$.  Indeed, 
                            
                                                                    
                                                                
          
the constants are controlled by the analytic-norms of the coefficients on a fixed larger strip and the corresponding Cauchy derivative bounds.

In the group cases the same estimate holds for negative times,
but only nonnegative times are needed below.

We start the Duhamel expansion in $\mathcal A_{\rho_1}$; since
$f\in\mathcal A_{\rho_0}$ and $\rho_1<\rho_0$,
$\|f\|_{\rho_1}\le\|f\|_{\rho_0}$.  Recall
\[
 \Delta_T:=\rho_1e^{-\kappa T}-\rho_*>0.
\]
The quantity $\Delta_T$ is the total analytic-radius reserve available
to the defect operators.

\medskip
\noindent\emph{Step 2: finite Duhamel expansion and weight bookkeeping.}
Duhamel's identity, with the perturbed propagator on the far left, is
\begin{equation}
 T_h(t)-T(t)
 =\int_0^t T_h(t-s)\Delta_hT(s)\,ds.
\label{eq:analytic-duhamel-bridge}
\end{equation}
Iterating \eqref{eq:analytic-duhamel-bridge} only through the leftmost $T_h$ factor and
using \eqref{eq:analytic-generator-depth-expansion} recursively produces a finite expansion
\begin{equation}
 T_h(t)
 =T(t)+\sum_{r=1}^{m-1}h^{2r}F_r(t)
   +h^{2m}\mathcal R_{m,h}(t).
\label{eq:analytic-finite-expansion}
\end{equation}
We now describe the two kinds of terms in \eqref{eq:analytic-finite-expansion}.

An ordinary coefficient word of total weight $r$ and length $\ell$ is
indexed by positive integers
\[
 r_1+\cdots+r_\ell=r,
 \qquad r_j\ge1,
\]
and has the form
\begin{align}
 \mathcal W_{\mathbf r}(t)
 :=\int_{0<s_1<\cdots<s_\ell<t}
 &T(t-s_\ell)R_{r_\ell}
 T(s_\ell-s_{\ell-1})\cdots \notag\\[-2mm]
 &\qquad\cdots R_{r_2}T(s_2-s_1)R_{r_1}T(s_1)
 \,ds_1\cdots ds_\ell .
\label{eq:analytic-ordinary-word}
\end{align}
Because every $r_j\ge1$, necessarily $\ell\le r$.

The remainder is generated by terminating a branch exactly when its
total weight reaches $m$.  More precisely, suppose a branch has already
accumulated ordinary weight
\[
 r=r_1+\cdots+r_k<m,
\]
and put $q:=m-r$.  At the next stage we use \eqref{eq:analytic-generator-depth-expansion} with this
remaining depth $q$ and select the terminal factor $Q_q(h)$.  The
resulting word is
\begin{align}
 \mathcal V_{\mathbf r,q,h}(t)
 :=\int_{0<s_1<\cdots<s_{k+1}<t}
 &T_h(t-s_{k+1})Q_q(h)T(s_{k+1}-s_k)\notag\\[-2mm]
 &\qquad\cdot R_{r_k}T(s_k-s_{k-1})\cdots
 R_{r_1}T(s_1)\,ds .
\label{eq:analytic-terminal-word}
\end{align}
The case $k=0$ is allowed and gives
$\int_0^tT_h(t-s)Q_m(h)T(s)\,ds$.  Since $q=m-r$, every terminal
branch carries the factor
\[
 h^{2r}h^{2q}=h^{2m}.
\]
Thus no word of weight larger than $m$ is formed: all ordinary words
have weight $r<m$, whereas every terminal word has weight exactly $m$.

\medskip
\noindent\emph{Step 3: compatibility of strip losses with the intervening
propagators.}
Consider first an ordinary word \eqref{eq:analytic-ordinary-word}.  Put
\[
 d_j:=2r_j+p,
 \qquad
 d:=d_1+\cdots+d_\ell=2r+p\ell.
\]
Allocate the strip reserve proportionally to the differential orders,
\begin{equation}
 \delta_j:=\Delta_T\frac{d_j}{d},
 \qquad
 \sum_{j=1}^\ell\delta_j=\Delta_T.
\label{eq:analytic-radius-partition}
\end{equation}
We start by verifying that these losses fit inside the available strip.
Without defects, propagation from radius $\rho_1$ to time $s$ leaves
radius $\rho_1e^{-\kappa s}$.  Suppose that by time $s_j$ the first
$j-1$ defects have spent losses $\delta_1,\dots,\delta_{j-1}$.  By
induction the available radius immediately before the $j$th defect is
at least
\[
 \rho_1e^{-\kappa s_j}-\sum_{i<j}\delta_i.
\]
After applying $R_{r_j}$ it is at least the same quantity minus
$\delta_j$.  Propagation over the next interval of length
$s_{j+1}-s_j$ multiplies the entire radius by
$e^{-\kappa(s_{j+1}-s_j)}$; hence the already spent loss is multiplied
by a factor at most one.  Therefore, at time $s_{j+1}$, the radius is
at least
\[
 \rho_1e^{-\kappa s_{j+1}}-\sum_{i\le j}\delta_i.
\]
At the final time $t\le T$ this gives
\[
 \rho_1e^{-\kappa t}-\sum_{j=1}^\ell\delta_j
 \ge \rho_1e^{-\kappa T}-\Delta_T
 =\rho_*.
\]
Thus the total reserve $\Delta_T$ may indeed be allocated among the
defects independently of their locations in time.

\medskip
\noindent\emph{Step 4: estimate of an ordinary complete word.}
Lemma~\ref{lem:analytic-defects}, applied successively with the losses
in \eqref{eq:analytic-radius-partition}, gives
\[
 \|R_{r_j}g\|_{\sigma-\delta_j}
 \le
 A_*B_*^{r_j}
 \frac{d_j!}{\delta_j^{d_j}}\|g\|_\sigma.
\]
Combining these bounds with \eqref{eq:analytic-propagator-bound} between consecutive defects
and using \eqref{eq:analytic-radius-partition} yields, for the integrand in
\eqref{eq:analytic-ordinary-word},
\[
 \|\text{integrand}\|_{\rho_*}
 \le
 e^{\Gamma t}A_*^\ell B_*^r
 \prod_{j=1}^\ell\frac{d_j!}{\delta_j^{d_j}}
 \|f\|_{\rho_0}.
\]
Since $\ell\le r$, the factor $A_*^\ell$ is geometric in $r$.  Moreover,
\begin{align*}
 \prod_{j=1}^\ell\frac{d_j!}{\delta_j^{d_j}}
 &=\Delta_T^{-D}D^D
   \prod_{j=1}^\ell\frac{d_j!}{d_j^{d_j}}\\
 &\le \Delta_T^{-D}D^D
 \le C_T^rD!,
\end{align*}
where we used $d_j!\le d_j^{d_j}$, $d\le(p+2)r$, and
$d^d\le e^dd!$; all fixed powers of $\Delta_T^{-1}$ and $e$ are
absorbed into $C_T^r$.

The simplex in \eqref{eq:analytic-ordinary-word} has volume $t^\ell/\ell!$.  Hence
\begin{equation}
 \|\mathcal W_{\mathbf r}(t)f\|_{\rho_*}
 \le
 C_T^r e^{\Gamma t}
 \frac{t^\ell D!}{\ell!}\|f\|_{\rho_0}.
\label{eq:analytic-word-integrand}
\end{equation}
For $0\le t\le T$, retain one factor of $t$ and absorb the remaining
$t^{\ell-1}$ into the geometric constant.  It remains to control
$D!/\ell!$.

Set
\[
 M:=d-\ell=2r+(p-1)\ell.
\]
Then
\[
 \frac{d!}{\ell!}
 =\prod_{j=\ell+1}^{d}j
 \le d^M.
\]
Furthermore,
\[
 \frac{d}{M}
 =\frac{2r+p\ell}{2r+(p-1)\ell}
 \le \frac{p+2}{p+1},
\]
because $1\le\ell\le r$.  Therefore
\[
 d^M
 \le
 \left(\frac{p+2}{p+1}\right)^M M^M
 \le
 \left(e\frac{p+2}{p+1}\right)^M M!,
\]
where we used the elementary inequality $M^M\le e^MM!$.  Finally,
$M\le(p+1)r$, so
\[
 M!\le\Gamma((p+1)r+1),
\]
and the remaining exponential factor is geometric in $r$.  Thus
\begin{equation}
        
 {
 \frac{d!}{\ell!}
 \le C_p^r\Gamma((p+1)r+1).}
\label{eq:analytic-ordinary-word-estimate}
\end{equation}
Substituting \eqref{eq:analytic-ordinary-word-estimate} into \eqref{eq:analytic-word-integrand} gives
\begin{equation}
 \|\mathcal W_{\mathbf r}(t)f\|_{\rho_*}
 \le
 t\,e^{\Gamma t}C_T^r
 \Gamma((p+1)r+1)\|f\|_{\rho_0}.
\label{eq:analytic-terminal-index}
\end{equation}
For fixed $r$, the number of compositions
$r=r_1+\cdots+r_\ell$ over all lengths is $2^{r-1}$, and the finite
number of structural defect types adds only another geometric factor.
Consequently
\begin{equation}
 \|F_r(t)f\|_{\rho_*}
 \le
 t\,e^{\Gamma t}C_T^r
 \Gamma((p+1)r+1)\|f\|_{\rho_0}.
\label{eq:analytic-terminal-prefactor}
\end{equation}

\medskip
\noindent\emph{Step 5: terminal words and the order-$m$ remainder.}
Consider a terminal word \eqref{eq:analytic-terminal-word}, with ordinary weight $r<m$
and $q=m-r$.  Give one half of the strip reserve to the terminal factor,
\[
 \delta_Q:=\frac{\Delta_T}{2},
\]
and distribute the remaining half among the ordinary defects exactly as
in Step~4.  By the Cauchy remainder estimate in
Lemma~\ref{lem:analytic-defects},
\begin{equation}
 \|Q_q(h)g\|_{\sigma-\delta_Q}
 \le
 A_1B_1^q\delta_Q^{-(2q+p)}\|g\|_\sigma,
\label{eq:analytic-terminal-integrand}
\end{equation}
provided $|h|\le\theta_*\delta_Q$.  Since $\delta_Q$ is fixed, the
right-hand prefactor in \eqref{eq:analytic-terminal-integrand} is bounded by $C_T^q$.

The ordinary part of the same word has total weight $r$ and is bounded,
using only the remaining reserve $\Delta_T/2$, by
$C_T^r\Gamma((p+1)r+1)$.  The full time simplex has length $k+1$ if
there are $k$ ordinary defects; its factor $t^{k+1}/(k+1)!$ is bounded
by $t$ times a geometric factor depending only on $T$.  Hence every
terminal word satisfies
\[
 \|\mathcal V_{\mathbf r,q,h}(t)f\|_{\rho_*}
 \le
 t\,e^{\Gamma t}C_T^{r+q}
 \Gamma((p+1)r+1)\|f\|_{\rho_0}.
\]
Since $r+q=m$ and $r\le m$,
\[
 \Gamma((p+1)r+1)
 \le \Gamma((p+1)m+1).
\]
The total number of terminal branches is at most geometric in $m$.
After enlarging $K_T$, we therefore obtain the central remainder bound
\begin{equation}
 \boxed{
 \|\mathcal R_{m,h}(t)f\|_{\rho_*}
 \le
 C_TK_T^m\Gamma((p+1)m+1)
 e^{\Gamma t}t\,\|f\|_{\rho_0}.}
\label{eq:analytic-terminal-estimate}
\end{equation}

\medskip
\noindent\emph{Step 6: insertion of the Chernoff step $h=t/N$.}
By construction of the second-order Chernoff family,
\[
 \mathcal S_2(t/N)^N=e^{tA_{t/N}}=T_{t/N}(t).
\]
Choose $N_T$ so that
\[
 \frac{T}{N_T}\le\theta_*\frac{\Delta_T}{2}.
\]
Then, for every $N\ge N_T$ and every $0\le t\le T$, the hypothesis of
\eqref{eq:analytic-terminal-integrand} is satisfied with $h=t/N$.  Setting
\[
 E_{2r}(t):=t^{2r}F_r(t),
\]
formula \eqref{eq:analytic-finite-expansion} becomes
\[
 \mathcal S_2(t/N)^Nf
 =e^{tA}f
 +\sum_{r=1}^{m-1}\frac{E_{2r}(t)f}{N^{2r}}
 +\left(\frac{t}{N}\right)^{2m}
  \mathcal R_{m,t/N}(t)f.
\]
Applying \eqref{eq:analytic-terminal-estimate} gives
\[
 \left\|
 \mathcal S_2(t/N)^Nf-e^{tA}f
 -\sum_{r=1}^{m-1}\frac{E_{2r}(t)f}{N^{2r}}
 \right\|_{\rho_*}
 \le
 C_TK_T^m\Gamma((p+1)m+1)e^{\Gamma t}
 \frac{t^{2m+1}}{N^{2m}}\|f\|_{\rho_0},
\]
which is \eqref{eq:analytic-bridge-estimate}.
<!-- end proof-24 -->
<!-- lemma-12: body_md -->
\label{lem:optimized-analytic-bound}There exists $C_T,K_*>0$ such that
\begin{equation}
 \|\mathcal M_{2m,n}(t)f-e^{tA}f\|_{\rho_*}
 \le C_T e^{\Gamma t}t
 \left(\frac{K_*t^2m^{p-1}}{n^2}\right)^m
 \|f\|_{\rho_0},\qquad 0\le t\le T,
\label{eq:optimized-preliminary-bound}
\end{equation}
for all $n\ge N_T$, with $N_T$ as in Theorem~\ref{thm:analytic-bridge}.
<!-- end lemma-12 -->
<!-- proof-25: body_md -->
For the even nodes $1,2,\dots,m$, recall
\[
 c_j^{(m)}=(-1)^{m-j}\frac{2j^{2m}}{(m-j)!(m+j)!}.
\]
                                                       
                                     
Then every refined step count
$N=jn$, $1\le j\le m$, also satisfies $N\ge N_T$, so the theorem applies
to every product entering the Richardson extrapolation.  
The exact weighted identity
\begin{equation}
 \sum_{j=1}^m\frac{|c_j^{(m)}|}{j^{2m}}
 =\frac{4^m-\binom{2m}{m}}{(2m)!}
 <\frac{4^m}{\Gamma(2m+1)}
\label{eq:richardson-weight-identity}
\end{equation}
then turns Theorem~\ref{thm:analytic-bridge} into
\begin{equation}
 \|\mathcal M_{2m,n}(t)f-e^{tA}f\|_{\rho_*}
 \le
 C_T(4K_T)^m e^{\Gamma t}
 \frac{\Gamma((p+1)m+1)}{\Gamma(2m+1)}
 \frac{t^{2m+1}}{n^{2m}}
 \|f\|_{\rho_0},\qquad 0\le t\le T.
\label{eq:richardson-weight-bound}
\end{equation}
Stirling's formula then yields \eqref{eq:optimized-preliminary-bound}.
<!-- end proof-25 -->
<!-- corollary-4: body_md -->
\label{cor:analytic-superfast}
Under the hypotheses of Theorem~\ref{thm:analytic-bridge}, choose
$\eta>0$ sufficiently small and
\[
 m(n)=\left\lfloor\eta n^{2/(p-1)}\right\rfloor.
\]
Then, for all sufficiently large $n$ and every $0\le t\le T$,
\begin{equation}
 \|\mathcal M_{2m(n),n}(t)f-e^{tA}f\|_{\rho_*}
 \le C_T e^{\Gamma t}t
 e^{-c_T n^{2/(p-1)}}\|f\|_{\rho_0}.
\label{eq:optimized-target-bound}
\end{equation}
Consequently the parabolic and Schr\"odinger-type second-order models
admit the rate
\[
        
 {\ e^{-cn^2}\ },
\]
while the third-order dispersive model admits
\[
        
 {\ e^{-cn}\ }.
\]
<!-- end corollary-4 -->
<!-- proof-26: body_md -->
The estimate to be optimized is \eqref{eq:optimized-preliminary-bound}.  Introduce the
quantity
\[
 \Theta_{m,n}(t)
 :=\frac{K_*t^2m^{p-1}}{n^2}.
\]
Then \eqref{eq:optimized-preliminary-bound} reads
\begin{equation}
 \|\mathcal M_{2m,n}(t)f-e^{tA}f\|_{\rho_*}
 \le
 C_Te^{\Gamma t}t\,\Theta_{m,n}(t)^m\|f\|_{\rho_0},
 \qquad 0\le t\le T.
\label{eq:optimization-log-bound}
\end{equation}
                                                                     
                                                                  
                                         
We claim that we can make $\Theta_{m,n}(t)<1$ uniformly in $t$, while the
exponent $m$ tends to infinity with $n$.

Since $t\le T$,
\[
 \Theta_{m,n}(t)
 \le \frac{K_*T^2m^{p-1}}{n^2}.
\]
Thus the natural choice is
\[
 m^{p-1}\asymp n^2,
 \qquad\text{equivalently}\qquad
 m\asymp n^{2/(p-1)}.
\]
We therefore set
\[
 \alpha:=\frac{2}{p-1},
 \qquad
 m(n):=\lfloor\eta n^\alpha\rfloor,
\]
with $\eta>0$ to be chosen.  Because $m(n)\le\eta n^\alpha$,
\[
 m(n)^{p-1}
 \le \eta^{p-1}n^{\alpha(p-1)}
 =\eta^{p-1}n^2.
\]
Consequently, uniformly for $0\le t\le T$,
\begin{equation}
 \Theta_{m(n),n}(t)
 \le K_*T^2\eta^{p-1}.
\label{eq:optimization-choice}
\end{equation}
Choose $\eta>0$ sufficiently small that
\[
 K_*T^2\eta^{p-1}<1.
\]
Fix any number $q$ such that
\begin{equation}
 K_*T^2\eta^{p-1}<q<1.
\label{eq:optimization-stirling}
\end{equation}
Then \eqref{eq:optimization-choice} gives
\[
 \Theta_{m(n),n}(t)\le q,
 \qquad 0\le t\le T,
\]
and therefore \eqref{eq:optimization-log-bound} yields
\begin{equation}
 \|\mathcal M_{2m(n),n}(t)f-e^{tA}f\|_{\rho_*}
 \le
 C_Te^{\Gamma t}t\,q^{m(n)}\|f\|_{\rho_0}.
\label{eq:optimization-exponent}
\end{equation}

It remains only to convert $q^{m(n)}$ into an exponential in the base
resolution $n$.  Since
\[
 m(n)=\lfloor\eta n^\alpha\rfloor
 \ge \eta n^\alpha-1
\]
and $0<q<1$, the function $x\mapsto q^x$ is decreasing.  Hence
\begin{align*}
 q^{m(n)}
 &\le q^{\eta n^\alpha-1}\\
 &=q^{-1}\exp\!\bigl(\eta n^\alpha\log q\bigr).
\end{align*}
Because $\log q<0$, set
\[
 c_T:=-\eta\log q>0.
\]
Then
\begin{equation}
 q^{m(n)}
 \le q^{-1}e^{-c_Tn^\alpha}.
\label{eq:optimization-final-exponent}
\end{equation}
Absorbing the {} constant $q^{-1}$ into $C_T$ and recalling that
$\alpha=2/(p-1)$, equations \eqref{eq:optimization-exponent}--\eqref{eq:optimization-final-exponent} give
\[
 \|\mathcal M_{2m(n),n}(t)f-e^{tA}f\|_{\rho_*}
 \le
 C_Te^{\Gamma t}t\,
 e^{-c_Tn^{2/(p-1)}}\|f\|_{\rho_0},
 \qquad 0\le t\le T,
\]
which is \eqref{eq:optimized-target-bound}.

For the two second-order models, $p=2$, so
$2/(p-1)=2$ and $m(n)\asymp n^2$, giving the rate
$e^{-cn^2}$.  For the cubic model, $p=3$, so
$2/(p-1)=1$ and $m(n)\asymp n$, giving the rate $e^{-cn}$.
<!-- end proof-26 -->
<!-- corollary-5: body_md -->
\label{cor:evolution-representation}
For each of the three analytic model classes, the solution of the
Cauchy problem \emph{(CP)} admits the Richardson--Chernoff representation
\begin{equation}
 u(t)=e^{tA}f
 =\lim_{n\to\infty}
 \sum_{j=1}^{m(n)}c_j^{(m(n))}
 \mathcal S_2\!\left(\frac{t}{jn}\right)^{jn}f,
 \qquad 0\le t\le T,
 \label{eq:evolution-representation}
\end{equation}
with convergence in $\mathcal A_{\rho_*}$, uniformly on $[0,T]$.
For the parabolic and Schr\"odinger-type second-order equations the
error is $O(e^{-cn^2})$, whereas for the third-order dispersive equation
it is $O(e^{-cn})$.
<!-- end corollary-5 -->
<!-- corollary-6: body_md -->
\label{cor:resolvent-representation}
Fix the extrapolation depth $m$ and let
$\operatorname{Re}\lambda>\widehat\omega_m$ as in
Proposition~\ref{prop:resolvent-rate}. Then
\begin{equation}
 (\lambda I-A)^{-1}g
 =\lim_{n\to\infty}
 \int_0^\infty e^{-\lambda t}\mathcal M_{2m,n}(t)g\,dt,
 \label{eq:resolvent-representation}
\end{equation}
and
\[
 \left\|\int_0^\infty e^{-\lambda t}\mathcal M_{2m,n}(t)g\,dt
 -(\lambda I-A)^{-1}g\right\|
 \le \frac{C_{\lambda,m}}{n^{2m}}\|g\|_{\mathcal D_m}.
\]
For the three original operators this gives explicit representations of the solutions of the corresponding nonhomogeneous ODEs:
\[
 \lambda f-a f''-b f'-cf=g,\qquad
 \lambda f-iaf''-b f'-cf=g,\qquad
 \lambda f-a f'''-b f'-cf=g,
\]
respectively.
<!-- end corollary-6 -->
<!-- proof-27: body_md -->
The parabolic case is immediate from
Corollary~\ref{cor:analytic-superfast}, since no change of
variables is involved.  Indeed, $\mathcal J=I$ and the fixed-strip
propagation of Proposition~\ref{prop:analytic-parabolic} corresponds
to $\kappa=0$.  Given
\[
0<\rho_{\rm out}<\rho_{\rm in}<r_a,
\]
choose intermediate radii
\[
\rho_{\rm out}<\rho_*<\rho_1<\rho_{\rm in}.
\]
The normalized estimate of
Corollary~\ref{cor:analytic-superfast} then gives directly
\eqref{eq:main-original-analytic-rate}.

We now treat the two nonsmoothing models simultaneously.

Fix
\[
0<\rho_{\rm in}<r_a
\]
and assume
\[
0<\rho_{\rm out}
<
\frac{m_p}{M_p}
e^{-\kappa T}\rho_{\rm in}.
\]
Equivalently,
\begin{equation}
M_p\rho_{\rm out}
<
m_pe^{-\kappa T}\rho_{\rm in}.
\label{eq:radius-gap-original}
\end{equation}

Because the inequality is strict, we may choose normalized radii
\[
\rho_0^y>
\rho_1^y>
\rho_*^y
\]
such that
\begin{equation}
0<\rho_1^y<\rho_0^y<m_p\rho_{\rm in}
\label{eq:normalized-input-radii}
\end{equation}
and
\begin{equation}
M_p\rho_{\rm out}
<
\rho_*^y
<
\rho_1^y e^{-\kappa T}.
\label{eq:normalized-output-radii}
\end{equation}
Indeed, \eqref{eq:radius-gap-original} is precisely the condition
ensuring that such a choice is possible.

By Lemma~\ref{lem:liouville-analytic-spaces},
\begin{equation}
\|\mathcal J_pf\|_{\rho_0^y}
\le
C_{\rm in}
\|f\|_{\rho_{\rm in}},
\label{eq:J-input-bound}
\end{equation}
because
\[
\rho_0^y<m_p\rho_{\rm in}.
\]
Likewise,
\begin{equation}
\|\mathcal J_p^{-1}g\|_{\rho_{\rm out}}
\le
C_{\rm out}
\|g\|_{\rho_*^y},
\label{eq:Jinv-output-bound}
\end{equation}
because
\[
M_p\rho_{\rm out}<\rho_*^y.
\]

By Proposition~\ref{prop:normalized-analytic-coefficients}, the
normalized generator satisfies the coefficient assumptions of
Proposition~\ref{prop:analytic-group} and hence of
Corollary~\ref{cor:analytic-superfast}.  Therefore, for
\[
g:=\mathcal J_pf,
\]
there exist $C_T,c_T,\Gamma>0$ and $N_T\in\mathbb N$ such that
\begin{equation}
\left\|
\mathcal M_{2m(n),n}(t)g
-
e^{t\widetilde A}g
\right\|_{\rho_*^y}
\le
C_Te^{\Gamma t}\,
t\,
e^{-c_Tn^{2/(p-1)}}
\|g\|_{\rho_0^y},
\label{eq:normalized-superfast-applied}
\end{equation}
for
\[
m(n)
=
\left\lfloor
\eta_Tn^{2/(p-1)}
\right\rfloor,
\qquad
0\le t\le T,
\]
and all $n\ge N_T$.

Since
\[
\widetilde A
=
\mathcal J_pA_0\mathcal J_p^{-1},
\]
and $\mathcal J_p$ is boundedly invertible on $L^2$, then 
                                          
\begin{equation}
e^{tA_0}
=
\mathcal J_p^{-1}
e^{t\widetilde A}
\mathcal J_p.
\label{eq:semigroup-similarity}
\end{equation}
By definition,
\[
\mathcal M_{2m,n}^{\rm orig}(t)
=
\mathcal J_p^{-1}
\mathcal M_{2m,n}(t)
\mathcal J_p.
\]
Hence
\begin{align*}
&
\mathcal M_{2m(n),n}^{\rm orig}(t)f-e^{tA_0}f
\\
&\qquad
=
\mathcal J_p^{-1}
\left[
\mathcal M_{2m(n),n}(t)
-
e^{t\widetilde A}
\right]
\mathcal J_pf .
\end{align*}
Using first \eqref{eq:Jinv-output-bound}, then
\eqref{eq:normalized-superfast-applied}, and finally
\eqref{eq:J-input-bound}, we obtain
\begin{align*}
&
\left\|
\mathcal M_{2m(n),n}^{\rm orig}(t)f-e^{tA_0}f
\right\|_{\rho_{\rm out}}
\\
&\qquad
\le
C_{\rm out}
\left\|
\left[
\mathcal M_{2m(n),n}(t)-e^{t\widetilde A}
\right]\mathcal J_pf
\right\|_{\rho_*^y}
\\
&\qquad
\le
C_{\rm out}C_T
e^{\Gamma t}t
e^{-c_Tn^{2/(p-1)}}
\|\mathcal J_pf\|_{\rho_0^y}
\\
&\qquad
\le
C_{\rm out}C_{\rm in}C_T
e^{\Gamma t}t
e^{-c_Tn^{2/(p-1)}}
\|f\|_{\rho_{\rm in}}.
\end{align*}
Absorbing $C_{\rm in}C_{\rm out}$ into $C_T$ proves
\eqref{eq:main-original-analytic-rate}.

For $p=2$,
\[
\frac{2}{p-1}=2,
\]
so the parabolic and Schr\"odinger models have Gaussian-type rate
\[
e^{-c_Tn^2}.
\]
For $p=3$,
\[
\frac{2}{p-1}=1,
\]
so the cubic model has rate
\[
e^{-c_Tn}.
\]
This completes the proof.
<!-- end proof-27 -->
